Honorable Sir:
Enclosed hereiwht a copy of a Petition to the
Senate and the House of Representative of the kegular session of
1935, Territorial Legislature of Hawaii, by the Sino-Korean
2
Peoples League, urging and asking for determining the facts and
information contained in the petition and also the Japanese
02
activities in general with the view of harnessing the Japanization
activities in Hawaii.
My spirit and motive of this petition is purely
for the sake of advancement of constructive Americanism in Hawaii.
I have been repeatedly warned by the secretary of
the Japanese Consulate Mr. G. Masuo, since December 1934. However,
I feel the American public is entitled to know the facts and I as
resident in Hawaii and America since 5 years of age (since l905).
I feel I owe my loyalty and obligation to the U. S. of A. because
it was under the Stars and Stripes that I had the privilege of
receiving my education and have received the opportunity to pursue
my life and happiness unmolested. America is my country and I am
deeply indebted to America for my very existence and life.
Therefore I believe every residents or citizens
should take up their duties toward the land and flag where they
are residing and enjoying the privileges as we do now in Hawaii.
As I have from time to time wrote you about Hawaii
and the Far Eastern questions, I feel particularly at this time that
I as representative of the Sino-Korean Peoples League should take
the initiative to inform the public of the facts and informations
with the view of determining the facts and informations for the best
interest of the U. S. and Hawaii.
The Japanese government from time to time are send-
ing all sorts of propaganda materials to Hawaii and distributed
among the intellectuals. As an illustration I am enclosing a copy
of translation of the tract "Peoples' Preparation".
-1-

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
The facts and informations contained in the petition,
the above quotation from the Japanese Naval press bureau tract
and the repeated warnings by the Japanese Consulate secretary itself
justifies my action in petitioning the Territorial Legislature in
instituting an inquiry and ascertain these facts and find ways and
means to harness the Japanization activities that hinders Americani-
zation program in Hawaii.
In view of the Japanese Consulate secretary's warnings I
feel that when this petition is presented on the 27 of March, they
may try to take action against me and question the merits and resi-
dence in Hawaii. Therefore I have given a copy to the agent of the
Department of Justice in Hawaii explaining my spirit and motive as
I have related.
I have also been indirectly warned and intimidated that
the Japanese government may demand my arrest and extradite me from
Amerioa to Korea. 1 do not know this is possible or not but on the
advice of the third deputy attorney-general office Territorial
government, he said as long as I have not violated the U. S. law
the Japanese cannot demand nor the U. S. will not deport me.
Whatever it is, I am leaving my fate to God and the fair
and just action of the U. S. government and I believe that God and
the U. 8. is fair and just, if the sacrifice of life itself should
remedy this deplorable racial situation in Hawaii, then I shall be
glad to do so for the sake of the security and peace of the United
States of America.
Due to the Japanese action I am forced to send you the
above information, so that in the event such things comes as they
have warned, I hope you will do what is right and just, but primarily
for the good of the U. S.
Thanking you for the privilege of writing you again and
trusting that if you see your way clear I shall deeply feel grateful
if you would reply my letter.
May God bless you, 1 remain,
Yours respectfully
Kilooo Haan
KILSOO HAAN
P.S. As stated in my previous letter the name W. K. Lyhan is a
given name by the League for me to go on during my work in
Hawai i.
enclosures: Peoples' Preporeduess
Petition

PEOPLES' PREPAREDNESS.
Avirtue to sacrifice oneself for others exists among personal
ethics, but among nations, there uoes not exist a moral that calls
for a sacrifice of one's own country. The members of the League of
Nations are working for international peace with the League as the
central wcrking point primarily to guarantee and advance their own
individual interests, and there is no nation who is working for peace
by sacrificing its own nation. The so-called friendship and concert
with otner nations, except on some special case, can be hardly reply
upon and in order to rank the same with the other nations, it is wholly
necessary for a nation to possess a self-reliant substance as its
final resort.
In an international meeting place, a n: tion without a thing that
she may rely on, no matter now just a thing nay be, she must either
depond on the majority or get the support of a powerful nation in
order to accomplish its views, and such nations's just voice sounds
like whine, but the voice of a strong nation is heard as a voice with
a thousand weights.
Even if Japan, shoulaering the future peace of the rast, desires to
dash forward for that principle with justice, if she has nothing that
she can rely on, justice will be called an injustice and will be forced
to meet a sad misfortune instantly. in connection with the manchurian
question, Japan was called insincere, but aue to the fact that the
other nations aid not resort to force because they were hampered by
the environments ana Japan nad a self-reliant substance, Japan barely
passed on the aays without a grief to this aate. That may be said to
have been a grace of the god and ii you think that we will get by in
that way in the future, you are greatly mistaken for it will not be
so. Hence, we all must strive extremely to perfect our national
defense and enrich the national strength so as to create a thing that
can be depended upon to force the worla to recognize a justice as a
justice.
As a whole, there is notning that will leave so much seeds for
future trouble as practicing inequality in making agreements between
nations. Among the nations that are counted as the woria's first
ranking nations, no matter what it may be, an unequal agreement is
not expected to exist.
Jepan who is the only great nation in asia and wno carries on
both shoulders the important mission of the peace guarantee of the
East due to her stablizing power in Asia cannot accomplish her mission
if she is restricted to an unequalled position comparing to the powers
of Europe and America, especially Great Britain and the united states.
Both the washington agreement ana the London agreement are, as the
people already know, unequal agreements that restrict our Navy armaments
to a lower ratio compared to those of Great Britain ana the united
States, and it is already plain how much damages the existence of those
unequal agreements have brought on the maintenance of the Hast peace
during the past several years. Reflecting on the ruture of Japan,
such an agreement must be firmly repulsed primarily for our national
defense and for international security.
Jepan is now in an unparalled advancement period, but the heavy
pressure of the powers are gradually increasing on her. The coming
disarmament conference is a test whether Japan will rise eternally
or fall. To be able to withstand this test is the people's real duty
to the virtuous "Show" (name for present Emperor's reigning period)
era and it is an honor and a happiness. wish that you will be con-
scious of what the whole nation's defense is and unite in one and
conquer the critical situation and dash forward for a great national
100 year plan.

In all international conferences Japan foilowed an iucntical

We are most concerned that the prcsent co perative netivities

Dr. Royama came to Hawaii under the payroll of the University
from the Japanese Imperial University of Tokyo, Japan; a member or
the Japan Counoil of the Institute of Pacific Relations. During his
tay here the representative of the Sino-Korean People's League had
the privilege of interviewing the eminent professor. During the
interviews. he expressed that "all the Orientals should unite under
the leadership of Japan in opposing the Cccidental powers in the
Far East and in the Pacific Area." He most emphatically stated that
the "Open Door Policy" should be junked and the Monroe Joctrine of
Asia for Asiatics be placed in its stead.
When jokingly reminued that his remarks and a titude were con-
trary to American policies and at least under the payroll of the
University of Hawaii he should be more careful, to which he replied
with a smile. ---
"Japan have gained her ground inch by inch by
hard persistent work using every opportunities no
matter how insignificant it may be.
When asked if he believed in the Americani ation of the Japanese
descents in Hawaii, he said:
From an Oriental to an Criental, do you think
the Japanese descendants can be made into Americans.
while Japan is at itss ascendency to world suprema-
oy?".
In returning to Japan, Dr. Royama is said to have made the
following remarks:
"The Cpen Door policy as it has been in the past
will no Longer be of avail to drtermine the destiny
of China or of the Far Fast. Such doctrine should
be denounced. ----
•The same is true in the case of naval disarma-
ment. Japan did what she thought justified but the
United States refused to recognize the adequacy of
the grounds on which Japan acted. some are apt to
describe the present situation as a cause of possi-
ble war between the two nations. while others re-
frain from pessimism over the situation, holding
that now that both parties have clarified their
positions from their own stanapoints, there should
be no more discussion. unless the United States is
inolined to interfere with the Japanese attitude.
Therefore the controversy is not likely to carry
thethe two nations to war.
'Nevertheless no one can guarantee that there
will be no war in the future."

JAPANIZATION ACTIVITIES IN GENERAL
Ex-Governor. w. y. wrear, once said:
"The most important problem of this Terri-
tory is the character of the chilaren who are
to become our future citizens. Whether Hawaii
will have a conmission form of government, or
whether she will be granted a statehood will
largely letermined by the character of our
future citizens. will these citizens of Crien-
tal descents act as th: U. . citizens or
subject of Japan or as citizens half-American
and halr-Japanese. is a most important problem.
About sixty percent of oir ublic schuol children are of Japanese
descents and mor than 95 percent o the e Jaganese chiluren attend
the Japanese language schools, ana about 37 percent of then are
airectly under the Budahist influence, where school teachers boldly --
"declared that they are teaching Japanese spi rit through the medium of
Japanese language". and they also ueclare that "Imperial rescript is
boldly read and taught".
Aside from the language school ., they are under the rigid family
system, their conduct and att tude rc loscly guarded by their mothers
and fathers.
Can we concci ntiously say that the Japanese during the past haif
a century made an honest ffort toward Americanization? r can they
be really and truly become loyal citizens if they are allowed to pursue
the present Japanize policy
The real and most oritical test poriod of loyalty and true
citizenship comes after school ho rs nd rticularly in the economic
field after graduation. The O-iental young men nnd wonen are at a
state of quandry after graduation, sonehow through circumstances their
close ties of as ociation with the things American and intimate contact
are cut losse and left to drift into the economic ficld without much
guidance or advice to compete wit the experienced businessman of their
race. Consequently almost O percent are forced b c: to the fold of
Japanese influence and powe . They are placed in a position in which

RESULT OF JAPANIZATION ACTIVITITS
Perhaps the most strixing result of Japanization activities can
be seen in the appalling number of dual citizens of Japanese ancestry
in Hawaii. The r sult of the Ixpatriation Law of 19z4 is particularly
a forceful illustration.
The intent of the rxpatriation Law was to ease the Americans and
to disarm them from doubting the sincerity of the Japanese government
and also to counteract the intense anti-Japanese feeling during the
years of 1921-l924. It was publicnlly stated by many Japanese leaders
that the Expatriation Law was comparable to an answer to a maiden's
prayer. The law enabled all the Japanese de cents numbering some
66,647 (June 30. 1924) to take out expatriation papers, thus casting
off the stigma of dual citizenship status. 1t was also said that the
children born thereafter will become automatically full fledged Ameri-
can citizens, unless they desired to remain Japanese subject. The law
was optional. However. it was hailed as the beginning of enlightened
friendly rrlationship between the two gr at people of the Pacific.
According to the stati: tics of the Japanese Consulate-General it
show that thus far less than 9 pero nt of the 66, 647 dual citizens
actually took advantage of the law. In other words only 5.768 expa-
triated. ven this small number cannot be honestly said that they have
voluntarily took such step because of their loyalty to Anerica.
Majority of these men and women are said to hive done so purely for
political and economic rensons.
What about the 3e.310 who were born since 1924? Are they techn1-
cally loo percent American citizens or half-American or hulf-Japanese?
Statistics show that cut of 39.310 up to June 30, 1934, the parents of
17.825 children or 45 percent have voluntarily registered at the Japa-
nese Consulate becoming dual citizens.
The net result of the Jxpatriation Law is a "Hoax", for today
we have 78.704 dual citizens of Japanese ancestry.

In Hawaii, four principal concealed methods are followed for Japan-
izing the minds of the American born. The Japanese subjects are reached
by yet another method.
(1) Well instructed Japanese are placed for years as servants in the
homes of wealthy men and leaders in business, educational and social
activities - particularly members of the anti-war groups. Through the
opportunities provided, an apt word here and there keeps the alleged
benevolence of Japan constantly to the fore. In addition, presents from
Japanese arrive at appropriate times. The American recipients become
completely hypnotized, even if not already predisposed to Japan. As
Professor Nitobe related to his compatriots after his close survey of
American psychology: "... there are Americans who defend Japan at almost
every turn, even at the risk of personal injury..."
(2) Propaganding professors from Japan are engaged to write Amer-
ican text-bbooks for American children. The motive was explained by
S. Aoki, secretary of the Society of International Cultural Relations
(directed from the Japan Foreign Office): "For several years the school
heads in Hawaii and America, with the aid of many influential educators
and missionaries, made a thorough survey of American textbooks for the
Japanese Society. The survey revealed that if proper propaganda is
systematically carried out in the schools, the American children will
become strong friends of Japan." (of. also activities, ▲
below).
(3) Americans of Japanese extraction are reached through the Jap-
anese private lan uage schools, and the religious organizations of the
Buddhists and Shintoists, and their social groups. By such means, the
most earnest efforts are made to instil wh-t is called "Japan or Mippon
Spirit" in the budding Americans. (Cf. enclosure 5, sub-heads Second,
Third, Fifth and particularly Seventh.)
(4) These Americans of Japanese extraction are also reached by
lecturers and "good-will" missionaries from Japan, who attempt to boulster
up the loyalty of t' ese Americans to Japan. Enclosures 9 and 10 are
illustrations. Cne of the lecturers, (enclosure Lθ) was already krown and
was expelled. There are others.
(5) Among Japanese generally, ho reside here, publications are
continually being received from Japan, di cussing the pending Japan-Unitd
States war, and referring to the aid from the 150,o08cjapanese in the
capture of lawaii. You have already received the account by Fukunaga,
endorsed by two very high Japanese admirals. Being printed in Japanese,
these works are seldom noticed by Americans in authority.

furthering Japanization artiities under the guise of promoting Japanese
culture. And yet, he was the an put forward by American educators and
others, at the Hawaiian statehood hearings bofore the Congressional Sub-
Committee in October, 1935, as the shining example of an American citizen
of Japanese extraction. In many ways they vere right.
It seems essential that the American Government take cognizance this
type of American citizen.

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.98)
Professor Greig Sinclair's discussion group at the home of
iss Ermine Cross. £979 Kalakaua Avenue. invited Professor Royama
in 1934. He stated that he had been sent to Hawaii by the Japanese
Cultural Bureau as a professor at the University of Hawaji and ex-
plained that this Cultural Bureau had been established by the Japa-
nese Government for the purpose of promoting Japanese culture to
the western world. He was among the first if not the first. selected
to oarry out the mission of Japan.
Jater part of the 16th century Japan began to use the mis-
sionaries and oducators as gencies of propaganda, as shown by
General Hideyoshi's cleverness in using the Portuguese missionaries
in the preparation to attaok Korea. The same principle was used in
1894-'05 -- 1904-'05. and etc.
From continuous observation and study. it is now clear that
the Japanese government. through ohuins of cultural societies and
other agenoies. plan to work on the minds of udolescents and young
adults of American citizens of any race, and so mould them in Japan's
favor that within the coming decade, the American clectorate will
support Japan's policies. in particular, the throwing opan of America
to Japanese labor. This may be recognized by any American who has
not allowed himself to be blinded by the insidious influences of
the Japanese. Future action in yielding to Japan's persistent pres-
sure must determine the Japanization or Americanization of Hawaii.
With sincere prayer I am presenting this letter for your
information with the traditional spirit of America's constructive
criticism for America's security.
Yours truly.
KILSJO HAAN.

Mr. Kilsoo Haan

Dear Mr. Haan:
Your letter of February 18 has been
received.
You may remember that a definite
effort was made a number of years ago (in
which I participated) to control the lan uage
schools.
This was frustrated by the courts.
I have been & member of the Institute
of Pacific Relations since its inception, I am
a Regent of the University, & Commissioner of
Public Instruction and a Trustee of Punahou
and I brand as absolutely false your statement
that those organizations are cooperating with
the Japanese Culture Bureau in any Jupanization
propagranda in Hawaii.
I see no relation between gaining an
understunding of a people with an appreciation
of its culture end approvel of its national
policies.
Because I studied German, admire
the achievements of German scholors, and enjoy
music composed by Germans I am not led thereby
to approve of Germany's policies leading to the
war, nor many of the acts of the Nazi regime.
In the same way some understanding of the Japunese
people end appreciation of their culture does not
lead me to approval of national policies.

Mr. Kilsoo Haan

Honolulu, Hawaii
Dear Sir
Your letter of February 18th was received in
due course. In it you charge the University
of Hawaii with taking an active part in a
program for the "Japanization" of Hawaii instead
of exerting its influence to build up and
strengthen American ideals. This charge is
utterly absurd. Friendliness and mutual respect
between nations must rest upon understanding and
knowledge of each by the other. Any contribution
which the University can make to this end is well
worth while. It seems to me that the Hymnof Hate
which you voice is directly in conflict with
American ideals.
Very truly yours
CHH:K

Koyooka Writin
Child's Hist
Dr. Eiichi Kiyooka annount
day that he is writing a book ok ge
history of Japan. designed primanly
for American children about10
years of age.
The history is being writte in
. y simple language. so that am:
mar school children may readily
nderstand the material, but Dr.
Kiyooka hopes that it will be in-
teresting to adults as well.
A portion of the first chapter has
already been completed and deals
with the legend of the sun goddess.
Amaterasu. found in the Kojiki. the
oldest book in Japanese.
Dr. Kiyooka will continue from
the prehistoric period. which deals
with gods and goddesses. to the
period of man. and will conclude
the history with the modern age.
He will also write an introduction.
which will explain what is meant
by the hustory of Japan and will
make comments at the end of cach
story exlaning its significance
Although he will return to Japan
by September of this year. Dr. Ki-
yooka hopes to have his book pub-
lished in America

lished the autobiography of Yuki-

Honorable Sir:
力
My contact with the Japanese Consulate have
been quite fruitful and in order to follow up to the final
.20211a
outeome. it is necessary for me to meet the Vice Consul
of San Francisco and a Secretary of the Japanese Consulafe
in Los Angeles.
No doubt you have received the photo-statio
copies whioh will prove my outline of the situation.
I hope if it is humanly possible, to be there
about January 15th as request ed by Mr. Secretary Iizuka of the
Japan Foreign Office. This is somewhat of a test they are con-
ducting in order to take me into their complete confidence in
the plans as I have stated in my letters forwarded to you.
I sincerely believe the work if properly pur-
sued and carried out I will succeed and will be a great value
to the United States.
,w
I have from time to time reported the progress
to Commander Kilpatrick of Hono lulu, U. S. Navy Inteeligence
Department. He has aided and cooperated with me to some ex-
tent. but as I have related. I cant carry on anymore without
大
funds especially when I have to leave for San Francisco and
Los Angeles.
As to my loyalty and sincerity I would like
to refer to Captain Willis W. Bradley. Jr. of Long Beach.
California, Secretary of Hawaii Arthur A. Greene, and Commander
Kilpatrick of U. S. Navy Intelligence Department in Honolulu.
I believe this is a valuable undertaking and
contribution toward preparation for National Defense.
-1-

=== Page 44 === (confidence 0.97)
I trust that you will give my request your just consi-
deration as soon as possible. I have been promised some finan-
cial assistance from the Sino-Korean Peoples Ieague. but due
to the present China situation, it may be along time before
they will be able to do so.
My immediate need is about $400.00 to get to San Fran-
ciaco after paying the necessary preparation in connection
with this work and thereafter whatever amount you think best
to keep me going for four to six months there. I believe I
will be able to accomplish this within the time mentioned.
Commander Kilaptrick thinks this is an important work
but due to lack of funds for such matter outside of his juris-
diction he cannot freely aid me financially. Particularly in
naval matters he has given me financial aid about $160.00
during the past three months.
I am trusting that you would soon give me the answer.
Yes or No. If I can't get any aid from you I feel I have done
my best.
Lay God bless you.
Yours tru ly.
W. K. Ryhan

Number 2, Introductory letter from Nr. Iizuka

The gist of Iizuka's Conversation.
I am wery happy that we will be able to try out the plans
which I have had in mind since my coming to Hawaii. You will find
Mr. Yonegaki understanding man. Be frank with bim in all your
dealings with him, be will give you the necessary ideas of the work
as we have planned. Once you prove your ability. we will trust you
to a greater project.
I have heard of the rumors about the island of Hawaii being
as a possible center of farming by the Japanese. As I have told you.
that the military people &lways have such bright ideas, but such
proposals are dangerous when the government takes radical actions.
Never mind about that it is the Oversea's department worry now. if
they can succeed. it will be fine. Be very careful about the iden-
tity of Nakamura -- anyway Nakamura is a lucky name -- it was Naka-
mura that gave a chance for Japan to take action in Manchukuo.
盈y
W. K. Rykan

CONFIDENTIAL.
My dear Me. Sesretary:
There are herewith enclosed copies of certain corre-
pondense, referred to this Department by the Mavy Depart-

894.20211A/6
California Joint Immigration Committee, in regard to an
alleged proposal by the "Department of Cultural Relations
of Japan" to supply certain educational material in the
nature of propaganda for use in the public schools of the
Torritory of Hawaii.
It seems likely that the organisation lest named is
the Kokusai Bunka Shinko Kai, or "Society for Internatiomml
Cultural Relations", a semi-offioial body sponsored in part
by the Department of Foreign Affairs of the Japanese Govern-
ment. For your confidential information there is hereto
appended an exeerpt from a report desoribing this organiza-
tion, prepered by the Ameriean Embassy in Tokyo.
In
The Honorable
Harola L. Iokes,
Seeretary of the Interior.

=== Page 48 === (confidence 0.95)
-8-
Im comsidering this matter, it would be helpful could
it be aseertained whether there is basis in fact for the
report that materials supplied by this organisation are to
be used in public sahools of the Territory of Hawaii. If
it should appear that this report is in agreement with the
fasts, it is suggested that an investigation be made in
regard to the nature and propriety of the materials to be
supplied.
It is believed that the Bureau of Education is in
position to conduet an investigation into this matter, and
it is therefore requested that such investigation be under-
taken disoreetly for the information and guidanee of this
Dopartment.

is reported that Miss Helen Gay Pratt, of the Department of
Public Instruction of Hawaii, 1s in New York at the invita-
tion of a unit of Teachers' College in conneetion with an alleged
project to have certain Japanese professors compile material
for use in American public schools.

WILL HAWAII SU PRESS INVESTIGATION OF

Honolulu, Apr11 19, 1935
On Maroh 27, 1935, a petition was presented to the
Hawaiian Legislature by . k. Lyhan on behalf of the
Sino-Korean Peoples League, asking for investigetion of
harges as to Japanese propaganda activities in Hawaii,
inoluding the introduction into the public schools of text
books covering Japan and her place in the sun, prepared
under Japanese direction. The petition was referred to
the Committee on Educat on, where, according to news re-
ports sent to the mainland, it wiil probably be pigeon-
holed. On Decem ber 11, 1934, the same organisation filed
a protest against use of such school text books with the
Commission rs of Public Instruction of the Territory, but
it received no publicity.
Lyhan states that demand has been made upon him by
certain interests concerned in the text: book olan and by
others (fearful lest agitation of the subject should
prejudice Hawaii's chance for statehood) for withdrawal
of the petition made to the Legislature; and that he has
been threatened with deportation as an undesirable alien
if he continues his activities in connection therewith.
He
has refused to comply with these demands, declaring that
the charges are substantiated by dooumentary proof, that
citizens of Hawaii and of the mainland as well are con-
cerned with learning whether such chargee be true or
false, and that he is prepared to defend his action and
ht status as a legal resident of Hawali.

A mmber of consideratiom ean be enmumerated as re-
sponsible for the establishing of the Sooiety for Inter-
nationel Cultural Relations: (1) the realisation that Japan
dees not enjoy a favorable press abroad, partioularly in
the United States) (2) the hope that dissemination of in-
formation concerning Japanese cultural subjects would lead
to a more sympathetic hearing of Japan's canse; (s) the de-
sirability of concentrating in one important body various
related efforts hitherto largely seattered and ineffeotives
(4) the wish of certain Japanese of liberal inclinations
to make an important contribution to the study of Japan by
foreign students by facilitating researeh in a manner whish
up to the present has been impossibles (5) the desire of the
same group to plase in competent and responsible hands that
part of the work of oultural relations with the Umited States
whieh has in the past besn promoted by a number of well-
meaming but unlettered Japanese enthusiasts; and (s) the
need for a contimuing cultural body in Japan to maintain a
somneotion with and thereby make of ourrent value the insti-
tutions which have grown up abroad as a rosult of previous
gifts (such as the Japanese library in New York).
The society maintains an office and library in the
business section of downtown Tokyo near the Tokyo station
(Marunouchi). The library was opened in Mareh, 19s5. The
chairman is Count Kabayama (Aisuke Kabayama, a graduate of
Amherst College, member of the House of Peers, and a liberal
who is well known to the Department). His most aotive
assistant is Baron Dan (Ino Dan, a younger man, lecturer
on Japanese art, son of Baron Takume Dan who as head of the
Mitsui interests was assassinated in 19s2). The society is
conduoting a series of lectures in Tokyo by eminent Japanese
aoholare

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.98)
is also under the suspices of the society).
The activities of the International Society for Cultural
Relations should not, however, be understood as being only
of a popularising nature. Its promoters are firmly cone
vinced that assistanee to foreign researeh students (whose
conclusions of course can not be controlled) will bear fruit
im the permanent national interest. In the near future an
American soholar (Dr. John Henry Wigmore) is to be aided
in researeh in the law of the shogunate period, and Count
Kabayame has high hopes that there will be no lack of sesh
work later on. In fect, it is the plan of the new institu-
tion to keep its head above the flood of the necessities of
routine oultural conveniences for foreigners and to devote
important aid to a few larger undertakings in turn as they
may be possible. It hopes to pay the cost of publishing
studies which would otherwise be impossible.
This organisation does not represent all the extra-
official activities promoted by the Foreign Orriee for the
good name of Japan abroad. The Foreign Ofrise contimues to
lend financial assistance to such purposes as the Japanese
Davis oup tennis team and motion picture news reels de-
picting Japanese subjects. In fact, the Embassy is informed
that the allotment for such purposes in the present fiseal
year is more than double the amount set aside for the Inter-
national Seciety for Cultural Relations. Nevertheless, the
Soviety represents an important effort to bring under uniried,
immlligent direction a considerable number of activities worthy
of future development.
The foundation is of course flooded with requests for
aid, the great majority of which are refused. In those par-
ticular instances which are found acceptable, support is
lavish. The special objeot 1s to promote a very few under-
takings at a time, in a manner more thorough and consequential
than would otherwise be possible.
As time goes on it is possible that scholars and in-
stitutions in the United States may be able to take more and
more advantage of the opportunities offered by this organisa-
tion. It is under soholarly dirootion of a notably liberal
tingo.

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.96)

opportunity to pursue research in many Japanese rieds which
have hitherto invited by their interest and discoureged by

fussd. A disoreet cholce of subjects should make it possible
to capitalize the obviously liberal inolinations of the
society's manegoment without running afoul of the institu-
tion's purse strings.

May 3, 1935.
Hon. Henry L. Roosevelt,
Assistant Secretary of the Navy,
Washington, D. C.
For Mr. Roosevelt's Personal Attention.
My dear Sir:
In the San Francisco Examiner, May 2nd, 1935, in an article written by
you, is found the folloving paragraph:
"For example, look into the schools in politics. They are vital. It is
in the schools and colleges that we prepere the citizen of tomorrow for his task;
and it is these young people who must receive and transmit the ideals which we would
have survive. And I conclude, reluctantly, that Americanism is not taught in our
schools to the extent that it should be taught."
You will be interested undoubtedly, therefore, in the following state-
ment of facts; there was presented to the Hawaiian Legislature on March 27th, a re-
quest for investigation of certain cherges, publicly mede and not yet denied, that
the Department of Cultural Relations of Japan, with approval of the Department of
Education of Hawaii, had arrenged for the use in the Public Schools of the Territory
of certain text books covering information concerning Japan, her history and objects,
prepared under direction of the Japancse authorities in part; and also that the inten-
tion has been announced of sending from Japan for use in the Japanese language
schools of Hawaii (wherein nearly 50,000 young Americen citizens of Japanese ances-
try ere taught, largely by Buddhist aliens) "educational material concerning the
vital national defense policy of Japen."
"Dentsu", a Japanese news agency, under date of May 3rd, and the United
Press, under date of May 15th, stated in telegraphed advices to the mainland that
this petition for investigation was quietly pigeonholed by the Senate Education
Committee to which it had been referred in order to avoid the stirring up of un-
necessary racial controversy and for the added reason that the Japanese are the
second strongest voting element in the Hawaiian Islands. Note also copy of letter
from the Chairman of the Education Committee of the Hawaiian Senate, May 18, 1935.

Very truly yours,

CALIFORNIA JOINT IMMIGRATION COMMITTEE

San Francisco, Cal.
Released
JAPANESE PROPAGANDA IN AMERICAN PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Japan is adding to her elaborate and frank system of propaganda in the
United States a plan thru which pupils in the high schools throughout this country
are to use text books prepared in Japan and giving the Japanese point of view on
matters national and international.
Protest was made to the Territorial School Commissioners of Hawaii by
the Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu, in a letter, December 1l, 1934, against
countenancing such a plan. In that letter it is stated that the plan was approved
at a Departmental Conference of the Japanese Government at Tokyo, November 21, 1934;
that there were present thereat the Department heads for Foreign Office, Education
Department, Cultural Bureau, Overseas Education Institution, Promotion of Interna-
tional Cultural Relations Society, and Prince Fushimi, Chief of the Japanese Naval
Supreme War Council, the founder of Hawaii's Prince Fushimi Scholarship Association,
incorporated in 1924.
Setsuechi Aoki, General Secretary of the Promotion of International Cul-
tural Society, said, "For several years the school heads of Hawaii and America with
the aid of many more influential educators and missionaries made a thorough survey
of American text books for the Japanese Society. The survey revealed that if proper
propaganda is systematically carried out in the schools the American children will
become strong friends of Japan."
Concerning the proposed free distribution of Japanese text books in our
public schools, Mr. Okada, the head of the Cultural Bureau and former Consul General
of Hawaii, stated "that all the necessary arrangements have been made with the Public
Instruction, also with the various religious and social leaders in Hawaii."
The plan includes also distribution among all the Japanese language schools
in Hawaii and America of reducational material concerning the vital national defense
policy of Japan", and also the distribution free of charge to the teachers of all
public schools in Hawaii of "reeding material, portfolios containing Japanese pic-
torials and other graphic materials for school room use, etc."
The letter of the Sino-Korean Peoples League concludes as follows:
"Undoubtedly you are aware of the significance of such projects in Hawaii. We are
very deeply touched and concerned with the action of the Public Instruction taking
a hand aiding in spreading Japanese propaganda in Hawaii. We believe the result will
be disastrous to Americanization of the American citizens of Japanese ancestry. God
knows they have enough contact with the things of Japan and the Yamato and Bushido
Spirit in their homes and in the language schools. Why let the Japanese Government
promote Japanism even in the public schools?"
Confirmation of these statements is found in a news item published Decem-
ber 3, 1934, in the "JapanTimes and Mail" of Tokyo, a newspaper maintained by the
Japanese Government for the purpose of presenting to readers of the English language
the Japanese Government point of view on current matters. The article is headed,
"High School Students in U. S. To Be Given Books With Correct Information on Japan",
and states: "The Society of International Cultural Relations at its meeting Monday
afternoon decided on a plan to be followed for the compilation of school text-books
to be used for diffusing correct information on Japan * * * * Material of instruction

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
JAPANESE PROPAGANDA IN AMERICAN PUBLIC SCHOOLS (ConT'd).
will cover almost every phase of modern Japan, embracing topics on the government,
economic and social conditions, art, etc., which will be presented in a way that
will appeal to the average high school student in America."
Japan's "International Cultural Bureau" is allowed 2,500,000 Yen in the
Foreign Office Budget for 1935, while the recently created "Society for Interna-
tional Cultural Relations" is supported by liberal contributions from Mitsui and
Mitsubishi.

San Francisco, Cal.
JAPAN'S PSYCHOLOGY AND AMERICAN INTERESTS
The Japanese people believe, their leaders including the frank and force-
ful Matsuoka, proclaim, and their school children are taught that Japan, as the only
nation whose ruler is Heaven-descended, is destined to rule the world. That belief
dictates the policy and acts of Japan and of the Japanese.
That policy received formal declaration at the ceremonial naming of the
new Heir to the Japanese throne when the Court Scholar, Dr. Ichimura, quoted from the
ancient classics this language: "The essence of ruling the people lies in the enlight-
ening of them. * * * If there are persons not yet properly governed, it is because
they have not yet benefitted by the Imperial rule. * * * If those who have not yet re-
ceived enlightenment under the Imperial rule are found they are to be subjugated."
A knowledge of Japanese psychology in such matters makes understandable the
calm ruthlessness with which Japan during the present century has defied protests and
ignored or violated agreements and treaties, and conquered and annexed adjacent terri-
tory and held it under commercial or military control in furtherance of her varied
interests; it will explain to some extent the air of placid superiority with which she
suggests that certain acts of other nations displease her sensitive people and should
cease--for instance the inexcusable act of the United States in floating its Navy on
the Pacific Ocean, and our recently announced intention to construct the Nicaragua
Canal,which prompted Japan to prefer a request to Nicaragua that she be granted
equal rights in construction and control of such Canal; it will explain why Japan
accepts compliance with demands of this character as recognition of the position to
which the Gods have assigned her and entertains corresponding contempt for the nations
which, as she assumes, thru weakness or fear, comply with the demands; it will explain
the calm assurance with which she openly propagandizes in this country for accomplish-
ment of her purposes and asks and secures the aid of American citizens and interests
in furtherance thereof.
It may even enable us to appreciate the humor in her latest move--a well de-
fined plan announced in the Japan Times of Tokyo, English langauge spokesman for
the Foreign Office, December 4, 1934, to introduce into our public school curriculum
a system of text books to be prepared in Japan with friendly counsel from a represen-
tative of the Institute of Pacific Relations, and designed to cover all matters on
Which Japan wishes this country to be "properly informed."
In connection with that plan the General Secretary of the Society for Inter-
national Cultural Relations reported that a thorough survey made for it by "the school
heads of Hawaii and America with the aid of many more influential educators and mis-
sionaries * * * revealed that if proper propaganda is systematically carried out in
the schools American children will become strong friends of Japan."*
The Sino-Korean Peoples League of Hawaii made, in December, a strong protest
to the Territorial Commissioners of Education against the introduction of such text
books, declaring that the result "will be disastrous to Americanization of the American
citizens of Japanese ancestry"; that "they have enough contact with the things of Japan
in their homes and in the Japanese language schools, and vhy promote Japanism in the
public schools."
Imagine the reaction in Japan if the United States announced preparation
by it of text books for use in Japan's public schools to "properly inform" Japanese
pupils on subjects of concern to us!
* C.J.I.C. Release #407, which will be meiled on request.

2-6-35

py of Item in Japan Times, Tokyo, Japan.
December 4, 1934.
HIGH SCHDOL STUDENTS IN U.S. TO BE GIVEN
BOOKS WITH CORRECT INFORMATION ON JAPAN.
The Society of International Cultural Relations at its meeting Monday after-
noon decided on a plan to be followed for the compilation of school textbooks
to be used at high schools in the United States for diffusing correct information
on Japan.
According to the plan, the collecting and provisional compilation of Material
on Japan will be undertaken by a committee in this country, while final compilation
will be done by a joint committee including both American and Japanese members.
It was also decided at the meeting to present the draft by the Japanese committee
to Mr. Lasker, chairman on the cultural relations of the I.P.R. ** for his sug-
gestions before sending it to the joint Japanese-Anericen committee for final
compilation.
Material of instruction on Japan will cover almost every phase of modern
Japan, embracing topics on the government, economic and social conditions, art,
etc., which will be presented in a way that will appeal to the average high
school student in America.
* The "Japan Times and Mail" published at Tokyo under direction of the Foreign
Office.
* I.P.R. - The Institute of Pacific Relations, whose members in recent bi-
ennial sessions - notebly at Banff, Canada, in 1933, were urged by the
Japanese delegation thereto to proselyte for immigration quota for Japan.
For further information in regard to this subject see C.J.I.C. Releases #407 - #408.

sed
JAPAN PROPOSES TO INSTRUCT OUR PUBLIC SCHOOL STUDENTS
The Japan Times and Mail, English language voice of the Foreign Office of Japan,
in its issue of December 4th last, published an interesting item headed, "High School
Students In U. S. To Be Given Books With Correct Information on Japan." The article
states that the Society of International Cultural Relations has adopted a plan for
compilation of text books for the purpose indicated, to be prepared in Japan by a
special committee with suggestions from a representative of the Institute of Pacific
Relations. The books will cover, it is stated, "almost every phase of modern Japan
. . . in a way that will appeal to the average high school student in America."
The Society of International Cultural Relations is an organization supported by
such powerful commercial interests as Mitsui, Mitsubishi, etc. It acts under direc-
tion of the Government Bureau of Cultural Relations, which has been allowed an appro-
priation of 2,500,000 Yen in the 1935 budget. "Cultivating cultural relations", is
Japanese for "inviting foreign approval of Japan's point of view". The Japanese dele-
gates to the Institute of Pacific Relations, at its recent bi-ennial sessions--and
particularly at that held at Banff, Canada, in 1933, as shown by news reports--sought
to secure the cooperation of American delegates thereto to obtain from the United
States an immigration quota for Japan.
USE OF THE BOOKS PROTESTED IN HAWAII
Further information as to the text book plan is found in & letter of protest
addressed by the Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu to the Territorial School Com-
missioners of Hawaii, December 11, 1934. Therein it is declared that the plan was
approved at a meeting of departmental heads of the Japanese government, including the
Foreign Office, at Tokyo, November 21, 1934. At that meeting, Mr. Okada, head of the
Bureau of Cultural Relations and former Consul General of Hawaii, is quoted as saying,
concerning the proposed free distritution of Japanese text books, that "all the neces-
sary arrangements have been made with the Public Instruction end also with the various
religious and social leaders of Hawaii."
Setuechi Aoki, General Secretary of the Society of International Cultural Rela-
tions, is quoted as stating that "for several years the school heads of Hawaii and
America, with the aid of many more influential educators and missionaries, made a
thorough survey of American text books for the Japanese society. The survey revealed
that if proper propaganda is systematicslly carried out in the schools the American
children will become strong friends of Japan."
The plan includes also distribution among all the Japanese language schools in
Hawaii and America of "educational material concerning the vital nationel defense
policy of Japan".
The letter of the Sino-Korean Peoples League protests against such proposed use
of the public schools "in spreading Japanese propaganda in Hawaii" and declares that
"the result will be disastrous to Americanization of the American citizens of Japanese
ancestry".
* Details in C.J.I.C. Releases #407-8-9.
#4102-13-35
Suggestion: Imagine the reaction in Japan if the United States were to publicly
announce its intention to introduce into the public school system of
Japan text books prepared under its direction furnishing "correct information" con-
cerning the United States and its policy and views.

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.99)
CALIFORNIA JOINT IMMIGRATION COMMITTEE
85 Second Street - GArfield 2697
San Francisco, Calif.
WILL HAWAII SUPPRESS INVESTIGATION OF
JAPANESE PROPAGANDA IN PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Honolulu, April 19, 1935.
On March 27, 1935, a petition was presented to the Hawaiian Legislature by W.K.
Lyhan on behalf of the Sino-Korean Peoples League, asking for investigation of charges
as to Japanese propaganda activities in Hawaii, including the introduction into the
public schools of text books covering Japan and her place in the sun, prepared under
Japanese direction. The petition was referred to the Committee on Education, where,
according to news reports sent to the mainland, it will probably be pigeonholed. On
December 1l, 1934, the same organization filed a protest against use of such school
text books with the Commissioners of Public Instruction of the Territory, but it re-
ceived no publicity.
Lyhan states that demand has been made upon him by certain interests concerned in
the text book plan and by others (fearful lest agitation of the subject should preju-
dice Hawaii's chance for statehood) for withdrawal of the petition made to the Legis-
lature; and that he has been threatened with deportation as an undesirable alien if he
continues his activities in connection therewith. He has refused to comply with these
demands, declaring that the charges are substantiated by documentary proof, that cit-
izens of Hawaii and of the mainland as well are concerned with learning whether such
charges be true or false, and that he is prepared to defend his action and his status
as a legal resident of Havaii.
The following outline of the case is gathered from statements, not yet contradic-
ted, which appeared in the petition to the Legislature; in the protest to the Commis-
sioners of Education, covering report of a meeting of certain departmental heads of
Japan, held November 21, 1934; in an item published December 4, 1934, in the Japan
Times and Mail, English language voice of Japan's Foreign Office; in an official re-
port of the Superintendent of Public Instruction of Hawaii, Oren E. Long, February 20,
1935, and in comment thereon by Lyhan in a letter of March 5, l935.*
The Department of Cultural Relations, Japan's instmment for foreign propaganda,
after careful survey and encouragement received from educational and church authori-
ties in the United States, decided thet "if proper propaganda is systematically car-
ried out in the schools American children will become strong friends of Japan."
Arrangements were made for use of such text books in the Hawaiian public schools, and
the initial volume is now ready for printing in Honolulu. The material therefor was
gathered in Japan under the direction of Okada, head of the Bureau of Cultural Rela-
tions, formerly Consul General at Honolulu, and with assistance from Bruno Lasker of
the Institute of Pacific Relations.The book vas compiled at Honolulu by Dr. Nisamichi
Royama of the Imperial University at Tokyo (who had three months leave for the purpose)
assisted by Miss Helen Gay Pratt "of the Institute of Pacific Relations and the Depart-
ment of Public Instruction".
It is charged that the plan also provides specifically for distribution in the
Japanese language schools of Hawaii of "educational material concerning the vital
national defense policy of Japan". Those language schools are attended by over 50,000
young Japanese, nearly all of them American citizens. The protest declared that use
of such propaganda in the schools will tend to further Japanization of Hawaii and
"will be disastrous to Americanization of American citizens of Japanese descent".

* Copies of these documents are in the office of the California Joint Immigration
Committee. * * * * C.J.I.C. Docs. #407-8-9-10 contain details of the matter.

N. S. G. V. OPPOSES STATEHOOD FOR HAWAII
At Vallejo, California, May 23, 1935, the Grand Parlor,
Native Sons of the Golden West, in annual convention,
passed unanimously the following rsolution.
WHEREAS, there is now pending in Congress a resolution introduced
at request of the Hawaiian Legislature to grant statehood to Hawaii,
WHEREAS, a similar measure of the Hawniian Legislature was vetoed
in 193l by Governor Judd, with the declaration that such a request should be
postponed until time had shown how the Asiatic majority would exercise its con-
trol of the franchise,
WHEREAS, two-thirds of the total population of the Territory is
Asiatic, the predominating element therein being Japanese, and two-thirds of
those Asiatics are American citizens by birth and entitled to vote as they
reach majority, while the entire Caucasian population is about one-tenth of
the total,
WHEREAS, 60,O00 or more Filipinos now living in the Territory,
plus such additional number as may be aimitted thereto during the next ten
year period under a special provision of the Independence Act, plus all the
alien Japanese now living in the Territory who entered under the Gentlemen's
Agreement (all such elements now barred from the mainland) would be free to
enter continental United States if or when statehood be granted to Hawaii,
WHEREAS, there is now before the Hawaiian Legislature & petition
asking for investigation of charges, publicly made and not yet denied, that the
Bureau of Cultural Relations of Japan, vith aporoval of the Department of Educa-
tion of Hawaii, is now carrying into effect plans for prop&pandizing in the
public schools through use therein of text books on Japan prepared under the
Bureau's direction; and for distribution to the 50,000 students in the Japanese
language schools (American citizens) "of educational material concerning the
vital national defense policy of Japan"; therefore
RESOLVED, by the Grand Parlor N.S.G.. that the Senators and
Representatives from Celifornia be urgently rejuested to vigorously oppose a
grant of statehood to Hawaii until such time as it may be shown that such
grant may be made with perfect safety to the security and interests of the
nation, and that the population of the Territory mey be relied upon for un-
swerving and undivided allegiance to the Union and its standards.

JAPANESE PROPAGANDA IN AMERICAN PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Japan is adding to her elaborate and frank system of propaganda in the
United States a plan thru which pupils in the high schools throughout this country are
to use text books prepared in Japan and giving the Japanese point of view on matters
national and international.
Protest was made to the Territorial School Commissioners of Hawaii by the
Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu, in a letter, December ll, l934, againet coun-
tenancing such a plan. In that letter it is stated that the plan was approved at a
Departmental Conference of the Japanese Government at Tukyo, November 21, 1934; that
there were present thereat the Department heads for Foreign Office, Education Depart-
ment, Cultural Bureau, Overseas Education Institution, Promotion of International Cul-
tural Relations Society, and Prince Fushimi, Chief of the Japanese Naval Supreme War
Council, the founder of Hawaii's Prince Fushimi Scholarship Association, incorporated
in 1924.
Setsuechi Aoki, General Secretary of the Promotion of International Cul-
tural Society, said, "For several years the school heads of Hawaii and America with the
aid of many more influential educators and missionaries made a thorough survey of
American text books for the Japanese Society. The survey revealed that if proper prop-
aganda is systematically carried out in the sohools the American children will become
strong friends of Japan."
Concerning the proposed free distribution of Japanese text books in our
public sohools, Mr. Okada, the head of the Cultural Bureau and former Consul General
of Hawaii, stated "that all the necessary arrangements have been made with the Public
Instruotion, also with the various religious and social leaders in Hawaii".
The plan includes also distribution among all the Japanese language schools
in Hawaii and America of "educational material concerning the vital national defense
policy of Japan", and also the distribution free of charge to the teachers of all pub-
lic sohools in Hawaii of "reading material, portfolios containing Japanese pictorials
and other graphio materials for school room use, etc."
The letter of the Sino-Korean Peoples League concludes as follows:
"Undoubtedly you are aware of the significance of such projects in Hawaii. We are very
deeply touched and concerned with the action of the Public Instruction taking a hand
aiding in spreading Japanese propaganda in Hawaii. We believe the result will be dis-
astrous to Amerioanization of the American citizens of Japanese ancestry. God knows
they have enough contact with the things of Japan and the Yamato and Bushido Spirit in
their homes and in the language schools. Why let the Japanese Government promote Jap-
anism even in the public schools?"
Confirmation of these statements is found in a news item published Decem-
ber 3, 1934, in the "Japan Times and Mail" of Tokyo, a newspaper maintained by the Jap-
anese Government for the purpose of presenting to readers of the English language the
Japanese Government point of view on current matters. The article is headed, "High
School Students in U. S. To Be Given Books With Correct Information on Japan", and
states: "The Society of International Cultural Relations at its meeting Monday after-
noon decided on a plan to be followed for the compilation of school text-books to be
used for diffusing correct information on Japan. * * * * Material of instruction will
cover almost every phase of modern Japan, embracing topios on the government, economic
and sooial conditions, art, eto., which will be presented in a way that will appeal to
the average high school student in America,"
Japan's "International Cultural Bureau" is allowed 2,500,O00 Yen in the
Foreign Office Budget for 1935, while the recently created "Society for International
Cultural Relations" is supported by liberal contributions from Mitsui and Mitsubishi.
#407 2-5-35

JAPAN'S PSYCHOLOGY AND AMERICAN INTERESTS
The Japanese people believe, their leaders including the frank and forceful
Matsuoka, proolaim, and their sohool children are taught that Japan, as the only
nation whose ruler is Heaven-descended, is destined to rule the world. That belief
diotates the polioy and acts of Japan and of the Japanese.
That polioy received formal deolaration at the ceremonial naming of the new
Heir to the Japanese throne when the Court Scholar, Dr. Ichimura, quoted from the
ancient classics this language: "The essenoe of ruling the people lies in the enlight-
ening of them. * * * If there are persons not yet properly governed, it is because
they have not yet benefitted by the Imperial rule. * * * If those who have not yet re-
ceived enlightenment under the Imperial rule are found they are to be subjugated."
A knowledge of Japanese psychology in such matters makes understandable the
calm ruthlessness with which Japan during the present century has defied protests and
ignored or violated agreements and treaties, and conquered and annexed adjacent terri-
tory and held it under commermial or military control in furtherance of her varied
interests; it will explain to some extent the air of placid superiority with which she
suggests that oertain aots of other nations displease her sensitive people and should
cease--for instance the inexcusable act of the United States in floating its Navy on
the Pacific Ocean, and our recently announced intention to construct the Nicaragua
Canal, which prompted Japan to prefer a request to Nicaragua that she be granted
equal rights in construotion and control of such Canal; it will expiain why Japan
accepts compliance with demands of this character as recognition of the position to
which the Gods have assigned her and entertains corresponding contempt for the nations
which, as she assumes, thru weakness or fear, comply with the demands; it will explain
the calm assurance with which she openly propagandizes in this country for accomplish-
ment of her purposes and asks and secures the aid of American citizens and interests
in furtherance thereof.
It may even enable us to appreciate the humor in her latest move--a well de-
fined plan announced in the Japan Times of Tokyo, English language spokesman for
the Foreign Office, December 4, 1934, to introduce into our public school curriculum
a system of text books to be prepared in Japan with friendly counsel from a represen-
tative of the Institute of Pacific Relations, and designed to oover all matters on
which Japan wishes this country to be "properly informed".
In connection with that plan the General Secretary of the Society for Interna-
tional Cultural Relations reported that a thorough survey made for it by "the sohool
heads of Hawaii and America with the aid of many more influential educators and mis-
sionaries * * * revealed that if proper propaganda is systematically carried out in
the schools American children will become strong friends of Japan." *
The Sino-Korean Peoples League of Hawaii made, in December, a strong protest to
the Territorial Commissioners of Education against the introduotion of such text books,
declaring that the result "will be disastrous to Americanization of the American cit-
izens of Japanese anoestry"; that "they have enough contact with the things of Japan
in their homes and in the Japanese language schools, and why promote Japanism in the
publio schools."
Imagine the reaction in Japan if the United States announoed preparation by it
of text books for use in Japan's public schools to "properly inform" Japanese pupils
on subjeots of conoern to us!
* C.J.I.C. Release #407, which will be mailed on request.

2-6-35

December 4, 1934.
HIGH SCHOOL STUDENTS IN U.S. TO BE GIVEN
BOOKS WITH CORRECT INFORMATION ON JAPAN.
The Society of International Cultural Relations at its meeting Monday
afternoon decided on a plan to be followed for the compilation of school text-
books to be used at high schools in the United States for diffusing correct
information on Japan.
According to the plan, the collecting and provisional compilation of
Material on Japan will be undertaken by a committee in this country, while
final compilation will be done by a joint committee including both American and
Japanese members. It was also decided at the meeting to present the draft by
the Japanese committee to Mr. Lasker, chairman on the cultural relations of the
I. P. R.**for his suggestions before sending it to the joint Japanese-American
committec for final compilation.
Material of instruction on Japan will cover almost every phase of modern
Japan, embracing topics on the government, economic and social conditions, art,
etc., which will be presented in a way that will appeal to the average high
school student in America.

JAPAN PROPOSES TO INSTRUCT OUR PUBLIC SCHOOL STUDENTS
The Japan Times and Mail, English language voice of the Foreign Office of Japan
in its issue of December 4th last, published an interesting item headed, "High School
Students In U.S. To Be Given Books With Correct Information on Japan". The article
states that the Society of International Cultural Relations has adopted a plan for com-
pilation of text books for the purpose indicated, to be prepared in Japan by a special
committee with suggestions from a representative of the Institute of Pacific Relations.
The books will oover, it is stated, "almost every phase of modern Japan . . . in a way
that will appeal to the average high school student in America".
The Society of International Cultural Relations is an organization supported by
such powerful commercial interests as Mitsui, Mitsubishi, etc. It acts under direction
of the Government Bureau of Cultural Relations, which has been allowed an appropriation
of 2,500,000 Yen in the 1935 budget. "Cultivating cultural relations", is Japanese for
"inviting foreign approval of Japan's point of view". The Japanese delegates to the
Institute of Pacific Relations, at its recent bi-ennial sessions--and particularly at
that held at Banff, Canada, in 1933, as shown by news reports--sought to secure the co-
operation of American delegates thereto to obtain from the United States an immigration
quota for Japan.
USE OF THE BOOKS PROTESTED IN HAWAII
Further information as to the text book plan is found in a letter of protest
addressed by the Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu to the Territorial School Com-
missioners of Hawaii, December l1, 1934. Therein it is declared that the plan was
approved at a meeting of departmental heads of the Japanese government, including the
Foreign Office, at Tokyo, November 21, l934. At that meeting, Mr. Okada, head of the
Bureau of Cultural Relations and former Consul General of Hawaii, is quoted as saying,
concerning the proposed free distribution of Japanese text books, that "all the neces-
sary arrangements have been made with the Public Instruction and also with the various
religious and social leaders of Hawaii".
Setuechi Aoki, General Secretary of the Society of International Cultural Rela-
tions, is quoted as stating that "for several years the school heads of Hawaii and
America, with the aid of many more influential educators and missionaries, made a tho-
rough survey of American text books for the Jepanese society. The survey revealed that
if proper propaganda is systematically carried out in the schools the American children
will become strong friends of Japan".
The plan includes also distribution among all the Japanese language schools in
Hawaii and America of"educational material concerning the vital national defense policy
of Japan".
The letter of the Sino-Korean Peoples League protests against such proposed use
of the public schools "in spreading Japanese propaganda in Hawaii" and declares that
"the result will be disastrous to Americanization of the American citizens of Japanese
ancestry".
* Details in C.J.I.C. Releases#407-8-9.

announce its intention to introduce into the public school system of
Japan text books prepared under its direotion furnishing "correct information"concern-
ing the United States and its policy and views.

sed
WILL HAWAII SUPPRESS INVESTIGATION OF
JAPANESE PROPA GANDA IN PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Honolulu, April 19,1935
On Maroh 27, 1935, a petition was presented to the Hawaiian Legislature by W. K.
Lyhen on behalf of the Sino-Korean Peoples League, asking for investigationof charges
as to Japanese propaganda activities in Hawaii, including the introduction into the
public schools of text books covering Japan and her place in the sun, prepared under
Japanese direction. The petition was referred to the Committee on Education, where,
according to news reports sent to the mainland, it will probably be pigeonholed. On
December 1l, 1934, the same organization filed a protest against use of suoh school
text books with the Commissioners of Public Instruction of the Territory, but it re-
eived no publicity.
Lyhan states that demand has been made upon him by certain interests concerned in
the text book plan and by others (fearful lest agitation of the subject should preju-
dice Hawaii's chance for statehood) for withdrawal of the petition made to the Legis-
lature; and that he has been threatened with deportation as an undesirable alien if he
continues his activities in connection therewith. He has refused to comply with these
demands, declaring that the charges are substantiated by documentary proof, that cit-
izens of Hawaii and of the mainland as well are concerned with learning whether such
charges be true or false, and that he is prepared to defend his action and his status
as a legal resident of Hawaii.
The following outline of the case is gathered from statements, not yet contradic-
ted, which appeared in the petition to the Legislature; in the protest to the Commis-
sioners of Education, covering report of a meeting of certain departmental heads of
Japan, held November 21, 1934; in an item published December 4, 1934, in the Japan
Times and Mail, English language voice of Japan's Foreign Office; in an official re-
port of the Superintendent of Public Instruction of Hawaii, Oren E. Long, February 20,
1935, and in comment thereon by Lyhan in a letter of March 5, 1935.*
The Department of Cultural Relations, Japan's instrument for foreign propaganda,
after careful survey and encouragement received from educational and church authori-
ties in the United States, decided that "if proper propaganda is systematically car-
ried out in the schools American children will become strong friends of Japan".
Arrangements were made for use of such text books in the Hawaiian public schools, and
the initial volume is now ready for printing in Honolulu. The material therefor was
gathered in Japan under the direction of Okada, head of the Bureau of Cultural Rela-
tions, formerly Consul General at Honolulu, and with assistance from Bruno Lasker of
the Institute of Pacific Relations. The book was compiled at Honolulu by Dr. Nisamichi
Royama of the Imperial University at Tokyo (who had three months leave for the purpose)
assisted by Miss Helen Gay Pratt "of the Institute of Pacific Relations and the Depart-
ment of Public Instruction".
It is charged that the plan also provides specifically for distribution in the
Japanese language schools of Hawaii of "educational material concerning the vital
national defense policy of Japan". Those language schools are attended by over 50,00
young Japanese, nearly all of them American citizens. The protest declares that use
of such propaganda in the sohools will tend to further Japanization of Hawaii and
"will be disastrous to Americanization of American citizens of Japanese desoent".
#420-A
*Copies of these documents are in the office of the California Joint Immigration
Committee. * * * * C.J.I.C. Docs. #407-8-9-10 contain details of the matter.

Subject:

The following extracts from a report are of interest.
"During the stay of the TAISEI  ARU at IILO, the
United States Attorney, Judge Ingram . Stainback, who
happened to pay a visit to the Island of Hawaii, observed
and reported as follows: While motoring on the west coast
of Hawaii in company with Governor Joseph P. Poindexter,
he observed a group of Japanese in uniform had set up a
sketching board at a point from which a lighthouse and a
section of the beach could be observed, and were engaged
in making what appeared to be a sketch map of that section
of the beach. Judge Stainback stopped his car, got out and
observed the partially complete sketch and identified the
group as personnel from the TAISEI MARU.
"The attention of the Japanese Consul-General at
Honolulu was called to the fact that he had failed to noti-
fy the Commandant of the expected arrival of the TAISEI MARU
in nclosure (J), and he was requested to furnish the Com-
mandant with a roster of officers attached to that vessel.
His reply, a copy of which is attached, Enclosure (I) avoids
reference to the fact of the early arrival of the ship and
does not contain information as to the status of officer
personnel on board."

FAASTERN AFFAIRS
The Secretary of State.
NOV1935
My dear Mr. Secretary:
894.20211A
In compliance with the request in your letter of July-1l
894.20211A/10
1935, File FE, 894.20214 I secured a mimeographed copy of a
statement entitled "Japan - Reference for High School Unit of
Work in the Study of Peoples of the Pacific Area". This is
evidently the educational material referred to as being supplied
by the "Department of Cultural Relations of Japan" for use in
the public schools of the Territory of Hawaii.
The Office of Education has made a detailed and critical study
of this manuscript with a view to its suitability for use in the
public secondary schools of Hawaii. As a result of this analysis
the following conclusions are submitted:
The material is not suited to the interests, abilities,
and experience background of pupils of secondary school age. Its
content is in large part concerned with abstract philosophical
tenets of Japanese scholars describing their social philosophy
but saying little about the effect, if any, upon the lives of the
people.
Introduction of specialized treatment of any foreign country
APR 11 1936
into the secondary curriculum is not in harmony with accepted
FILED
objectives of secondary schools of the United States. These ob-
jectives are general rather than specific, and their aim is
toward effective membership in society for all pupils and per-
petuation of traditional culture.
This manuscript 1s probably intended to be historical in
character. Prevailing practice in secondary schools of the
United States tends toward broadening the social studies field
through the introduction of sociological, economic and biological
studies rather than more history. Two years of history are generally
7

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
offered - American history and World history. Even English
history, formerly an offering in secondary schools, was offered
in only a fraction of one percent of secondary schools of the
United States in 1930. While there may be justification for
giving more attention to the history of the countries bordering
the Pacific Ocean in Hawaii than in continental schools, in line
with prevailing practice this should be done as a part of and in
connection with World history rather than through studies of each
of the countries as separate and distinct units. The secondary
school course is not long enough to offer such specialized courses
and also complete the courses directly in line with the achieve-
ment of secondary school objectives.
Considerable space is given to literature and art. This would
seem an excellent feature if it were not for the fact that there is
a good deal that is unimportant, if not trivial; summaries of
Japanese novels, for example, too brief to be of value in judging
their quality and giving no clear idea of either the type of
literature characteristic of the time or its influence on the lives
of the people. Much of the philosophy is abstract and difficult to
understand and seems quite unsuited to the interests of secondary
school pupils. Very little space is given to descriptions of the
life of the people, to creating an understanding of their govern-
ment, industries, and ways of living.
Prevailing practice in history teaching in the United States
places emphasis on political and economic aspects of history.
Phases of cultural history are regarded as incidental and supple-
mentary. It seems desirable for high schools in Hawaii to follow
established practices in the United States unless practice indis-
putably better is assured. The manuscript on Japan treats chiefly
of the cultural aspects of the ruling classes in Japan. There is
practically nothing in it about the political and industrial life
of the mass of the people.
As a whole the manuscript smacks of rather subtle propaganda.
Questionable activities are condoned or explained, or parallel
activities in other countries are mentioned, apparently to show
that Japan, when engaged in questionable undertakings, was no
worse than other countries. Expansion by the Japanese in Manchuria
and Korea is for stability and protection, unlike that in other
countries which is for conquest and aggrandizement. On the other
hand, the authors do not hesitate to censure the United States.
For example, the law forbidding entry of Japanese into the United
States is criticized as "one of the most indefensible and un-
-2-

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.98)
fortunate pieces of legislation imaginable." Japan is said
to have scrupulously observed its agreement while the United
States is represented as abruptly breaking it.
I am forwarding the manuscript, together with copy of a
letter from Mr. Oren E. Long, Superintendent of Public Instruction
of the Territory of Hawaii, should you desire to make a further
review.
Sincerely yours,
HavelEches
Secretary of the Interior.
Enclosure 733647.
