TERRITORY OF HAWAII
DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC INS TRUCTION
HONOLULU
ren E. Long
July 20, 1935.
Superintendent.
Dr. Ernest Gruening,
Director, Division of Territories and Island Possessions,
Department of the Interior,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
At the request of Governor Poindexter, I am submitting,
under separate cover by air mail, a mimeographed copy of a state-
ment entitled "Japan - Reference for a High School Unit of Work
in the Study of Peoples of the Pacific Area".
This mimeographed statement, which is only a preliminary
draft of what will ultimately be a published volume, is by Miss
Helen Gay Pratt, for many years a teacher in the public schools
of Hawaii and at Teachers College, University of Hawaii. While
working on this, Miss Pratt consulted many sources and had the
active assistance of Dr. Masamichi Royama, visiting professor
at the University of Hawaii during the second semester of the
school year 1933-34, Dr. Gregg Sinclair, associate professor
of English, University of Hawaii, and teachers from three pri-
vate high schools in Honolulu and two public high schools.
During the school year 1934-35, the manuscript was
used on an experimental basis in each of the following Honolulu
schools:
Punahou Academy, private high school;
Kamehameha School for Girls, private high school;
Kamehameha School for Boys, private high school;
McKinley High School, public high school; and
Roosevelt High School, public high school.
On the basis of this use, the manuscript is now being revised. It
will appear again in mimeographed form. After further experimental
use, it is expected there will be a preliminary printing similar
to a book prepared locally on China entitled, "China: Whence and
Whither?", after which it is hoped that it will be accepted by one
of the outstanding mainland publishing companies for regular pub-
lication.
For your information, I wish to point out that the work on
Japan is one of a number of titles which have been under considera-
tion for a period of about four years. Teachers of the Public and
private schools of Hawaii have been somewhat appalled at the lack of

=== Page 106 === (confidence 0.99)
authentic material bearing on the history of peoples living in the
Pacific area. In the absence of suitable textbooks, they set them-
selves the task of producing them. So far, they have actively
attacked five studies: Hawaii, China, Japan, Russia and the
Philippine Islands. Two books on Hawaii were published in l933.
These were: Ancient Hawaiian Civilization, edited by Miss Pratt,
and Hawaii and Its People by Miss Lorna Jarrett, instructor of
Geography at the University of Hawaii. Copies of these books
are being forwarded to you under separate cover. A book on the
Philippine Islands by Professor Kiesing, of the University of
Hawaii, is nearing completion and will doubtless be published
in the near future. Reference has already been made to the
book on China. After a mimeographed copy of the manuscript had
been used in a number of secondary schools during the year
1933-34, a preliminary printing was made for experimental use
during the year 1934-35. This has been well received both in
Hawaii and by a number of schools on the mainland. Criticiams
and suggestions have been received from many sources. On the
basis of these, the manuscript is now being re-edited for final
publication. The changes will be quite radical, both from the
standpoint of content and organization. The principal criticism
of the content is that sufficient space has not been given to the
life lived by the common people of China.
The work on Japan is undergoing a similar evolution. If
it proves to be as successful as the other books have been, we
will expect to use it in the public schools of the Territory.
Furthermore, there is every reason to think that there will be a
demand for it on the mainland. This, of course, will depend upon
the merit of the book and more particularly upon the ability of
those who are responsible for it to handle the present-day interna-
tional policy of Japan in a fair and constructive way.
In the final draft, there will doubtless be more space
devoted to the cultural background of Japan and to the life lived
by the masses of the people. It is not felt that any conclusive
statements can be made about current international developments.
This 1s touched upon at the top of Page 169 in the following brief
paragraph:
"The situation in the Far East changes daily,
and the international relationships of Japan are
modified very rapidly. The daily papers reflect
these changes. There is little that is stable in
the present-day world, and of all the unstable
situations, that in the Far East is one of the
most unpredictable."
The authors, however, had not hesitated to discuss develop-
ments of the past few years. The policy of expansion, for instance,
is referred to by quotation as "not only morally objectionable, but ....
-2-

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
inevitably unpopular in the territories absorbed as it leads not
only to political subservience but to occupation by an alien
garrison, and the native population regards the garrison, wich
much reason, as a means of suppressing their liberties rather than
defending their frontiers." (Page l47). The lack of any sound
economic basis for this policy of expansion is emphasized in a
statement beginning at the top of Page l62, and the attitude of
other nations is referred to as follows on Page l68: "The world
at large condemned this advance into Manchuria". An international
disapproval is further emphasized in the last paragraph beginning
on Page 194.
Further study is being given to statements that appear on
Page 150. Doubtless important changes should be made in the last
paragraph of this page.
I wish to emphasize that this Department is interested in the
book on Japan and the others referred to only from the standpoint of
securing the best possible instructional materials for the children
of our schools. There has been a nation-wide recognition during the
past few years that the Pacific basin generally has been neglected
in our school textbooks. An examination of any number of present-day
histories show this to be true. We have felt that Hawaii is in a strategis
position to make a contribution in this particular field. We recog-
nize, however, that any production will have to stand on its own
merit and will be subjected, particularly anything on Japan, to the
most searching investigation on the part of different groups, both
locally and on the mainland. It is for this reason that we are glad
to submit the mimeographed manuscript on Japan, with the request that
it be criticized by the Research Department of your Division.
Sincerely yours,
(Sgd.) OREN E. LONG
Superintendent.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
APR 8 1990cember 27, 1935.
FE
DIVISION OF
Mr.Tarner:
I have read the manuscript on Japan transmitted
with Mr. Ickes' letter of November 27, 1935, and
F
believe that the comments made in his letter are
H
quite sound.
894.20211A/
Although it is both natural and reasonable that
students of the Japanese race in the Hawaiian Islands
should be given an opportunity in the public schools
to acquire some special knowledge of the land of their
racial origin, it seems to me that this manuscript
bends over backward in its desire to achieve this
purpose and loses sight of the fact that the aim of
0
the public schools cannot be other than to melt the
students of the various races of Hawaii into bona fide
Americans. This manuscript not only fails to promote
that end, but definitely obstructs it. I am reluctant
to believe that, in its preparation, there was any
intention to further "pernicious propaganda"; but
there runs through it all the evidences of a Japanese
APR81
shading that cannot be erased by removing excerpts
here and there; for the Japanese toning pervades the
whole product. There is perhaps nothing very wicked
F
or dangerous in this per se; and I should not perceive
/FG
any very great objection to the work being placed in

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
public libraries, where it might be available as
representing a view point at least partially Japanese;
but I do think this fact is a sufficient reason for
its rejection from the public schools as a text book.
In detailed criticism, it may be said that a very
large part of this material is quite unsuited to students
of high school age and that portions of it would be
of questionable utility even for students in elementary
college courses. A goodly portion of the manuscript
could be deleted to much advantage. The summaries
and extracts from novels and plays are long enough
to occupy much space, but too short to give any
worth while idea of the works from which they are
taken. Much of the philosophy is beyond the powers
of students in secondary schools. The portions
dealing with modern Japanese politics, although
containing for the most part correct statements of
fact, abound in half truths and amount to little more
than special pleading.
The question of the extent to which students
of the Japanese race in Hawaii should receive special
instruction about Japan is perhaps one more suitable
to be passed upon by the Hawaiian authorities. If
high school children of the Italian race in New York
City were given a similar amount of special in-
struction about Italy, I should feel that it was

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.95)
-3-
xcessive in relation to the time at their disposal
for acquiring the usual American high school education.
Notwithstanding the foregoing criticism, the
reading of the manuscript has not been an uninteresting
task; and I should by no means recommend that it be
cast into the fire and burned. But I do think that
the thregd of Japanese silk running through the
whole work renders it unsuitable for use by those
who are expected, I presume, to become American
citizens in fact as well as in name.
M.
HA:MFP:LEG

PREFACE
This survey of the Philippine islands and their people has been
drafted as an experimental text for high school use. It was done under the
auspices of thc department of unthropology in the University of Hawaii, at the
request of the Huw ii Group of the Instituto of Pacific Relations.
The difficulties of trying to intorpret the life and problems of
several million people in sc small a space ure obviaus.
On the other hand,
there is as yet  short and comprehensive work available dealing with the
Filipinos. This survey attempts to give a sympathotic, scientifically
correct picture of the peopling of the islands, the successive Spanish and
American rule, and the problems and possibilities that face the Filipinos
as they start out upon their trial independence. A bibliography at the end
contains references to standard works on the Philippines which will fill out
the picture in greater detail.
It will be understood that the interpreta-
tion of Philippine mutters givon here is not necessarily subsoribed to by the
institutions concerned, but is untirely the writer's own.
.Any comments
and oriticisms that teuchers are willing to make us a result of using the
material would be greatly welcomed. hs noted, it is an experimental draft.
The writer must cknowledge his indebtedness to the Institute of
Paoific Relations, under the auspices of which ficld studics in the Philip-
pines were done; t, m ny friends in the Philippines, notably Professor H.
Otley Beyer of the University of the Philippines; to colleagues and teachers
in Hawaii who ruad the manuscript and made veluable suggestions; to the

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
publishers of the Philippine Magazine, Manilu, for permission to quote copyright
material; and to the writer's wife, Marie M. Keesing, who collaborated
throughout the study.

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
CHAPTER I
WHY STUDY THE FILIPINOS?
What do we know of the Filipinos and their oountry? What are their
racial baokgrounds, their characteristics as a people, and their ways of living?
Why have many thousands of them come across to Hawaii and the American mainland?
How did their country become a possession of the United States, and why are they
now asking for their national independence?
A class of Amorican university students were asked recontly what was the
population of the Philippinc Islands. Their answers rungod from four thousand to
onc hundrcd and fifty million. Fow could givo thc names of any of the islands, and
praotioally nonc could namo a city or town there other than the capital, Manila.
Most of us, whether in school or grown up, would have to confoss likowisc that our
ideas are vaguc about Unclc Sam's largest insular possession.
We have all seen muoh in the newspapers and magazines as to whether or not
the Filipinos are ready to be made independent. Many older folk can recall how
Admiral Dewey took the islands from Spuin in 1898, and thousands of American
soldiers went out to fight those omong the "little brown brothers" who objected to
United States rule. Today we roul how people in some states of the Union resent the
Filipinos coming in as cheup luborurs. Yet it is caid that ninety per cent of
Americans would not recognize & Filipino us apart from other Oriental peoples or
else Latin Americans.
Why should we want to know the Filipinos and their story? In the first
place, Americans cannot escape a senso of being responsible for the welfare of the
Filipino people in view of the fact that they have beon wards of the United States
for more than thrce decades. Even when their indopendence comes, as Congress has
docreed, they will continue to look aoross the Pacific for guidance in eoonomio,

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
educational and other matters; the marks of Americanization cannot be wiped out.
For this reason, and also to understand better the thousands of Filipinos who have
come overseas, a knowledge of this island people will be useful.
Furthermore, their position in the Orient and Pacific is unique. Geographi-
cally and to a large extent racially they belong to the Orient. Yet the great
number of then huve been Christian for some threc and a half centuries, and through
the succussive Spanish and Amorican rule of their country they have come to identify
themselves largely with the Occident. With frcolom at hand, will they turn back to
the East, or try to westernize themselvos mor, or work out some speoial way of
life between the two thut will bo a kind of bridgc between East and West? Again,
will independonce be of benefit to them and to the wider world, in these days of
international troubles and rivalries in thc Pucific? Their island home is in many
parts underpopulated or cmpty, and nearby are crowded lunds. Its rioh resources are
little developed commorcially, and that largely by uericans, Chineso, Japanese and
other outsiders. Will the historiun of the future be able to write of the Filipino
nation that it has "made good," and of American guardianship and withdrawal that it
has been both wise an? generous?
Finally, the Philipnine islands huve roun one of Nature's most fasoinating
experimental laboratories in which sh is trying out new combinations of human
traits. Hawaii is ofton spoken of as a "melting pot." But the Philippines have
been a molting pot of races and cultures for far longer, in fact for thousands of
years. They seen to have caught somo of cvery kind of  olk that have moved castward
from Asia or roved the Pocific sons - ancient peoples like the pigmy Negritos, early
peoples called Indonesians reluted to the white races, Malayans who are more Asiatic,
Hindus from India, Arabs, Chinese, Japanese, Spaniards, Mexicuns and now Americans.
It may be that if all humanity tall, short, whito, yollow, brown and black, could b
shaken together into one whole, the result would be something like the Filipino

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
people now in the making.
As with race, so also it is with language, custom and belief, that is,
with culture. The Filipinos of today have acquired from their forefathers some
eighty or so dialects more or less mutually unintelligible; many know Spanish, and
now English is becoming the main common language. Though most are agriculturalists
there are still wundering peoples in tho deeper forcsts and mountains who hunt much
as did folk in the early days of man's development. One out of every eleven
Filipinos is either a Mohammeden, or a so-callcd "pagan" who worships ancestors and
the spirit forces of nature. While many Filipinos are educated to the point of
being virtually American in outlook and tastes, others even yet are hardly aware of
themselves as "Filipino," knowing littlc of aff irs outside their local villages and
traditional ways. It is a land of rich cultural diversity and mingling.
The Spanish conquorors named the islands Las Filipinas in honor of their
Crown Prince who later becamo Philip II. In English usagc this became the
Philippines (noto the one "1" and two "p's" and not vice versa). Following the
Spanish mode of exprossing gender, we speak today of any male among the local people
as a Filipino and of any femalc as a Filipina. The Spaniards themselves used to
refer to the islunders more usually us los Indios, or Indians. This was not becauso
of the actual fact that a considerable proportion of their blood traced back to
India, but rather that the term which was mistakenly applied by Columbus to the
peoples of the Americas was again transferred by the Spaniards from America to the
Philippines. When in modern days, a consciousness of a common nationhood began to
stir among the Philippine folk, they put aside the term Indio as bearing a stigma
and oalled themselves instcad Filipinos.

CHAPTER II
A "ROSARY OF GLOWING ISLANDS"
To understand the life of the Filipino or of any other people, it is
essential to know the land in which they dwell. Men have needs for food, clothing,
shelter, tool, and the like whioh make them directly dependent on Nature, or, as
the scientist calls her, the physical environment. The climute, type of country,
and natural rosources affect not only the physique of the inhabitants but also
their customs and ideas. True, humans have loarnt to counter somewhat the effects
of heat and cold, to control soil und wator for ugriculture, to brced plants and
animals, work minorals, and hurnoss clectricity. That is, they have fashioned for
themselves a "seconlary environnt" in which to live more comfortably and safely.
This, however, is no congu st of nature us somo aro wout to boast. Rather it is
developing tenm-vork with nature; ordering her by obeying her orders. We cannot
know the Filipino without exumining at the sturt the physical environment of the
Philippine Islands.
As with any visitor to un unfamiliar placc, the best rule is to look at a
map. Could wo drew a map of the Thilinpinos fron momory? It would not be casy, for
there are estimated to be 7,O33 islands large and small in the archipelago. The
most convenient way to rcmember the group is to cce it first as a rough triangle,
within which the islands appear not unlike tho bony skoleton of some squatting
animal (see skotch map 1). This triangle or skel ton falls into four main parts.
1. In the north is the largost island, Luzon. Off its west coast is a
smaller one named Mindoro, while northernmost of all are two groups of islands
called the Babuyanes an! Butanes.
2. In the southeast is the socond ltrgcst island, Mindanao. A string of
islands running southwost from it toward Borneo is known us the Sulu Arohipelago.

3. Between Luzon and Mindanao are a cluster of large and small islands
kmown collectively as the Visayas. The most important in terms of size are Samar,
Negros, Panay, Leyte, Cebu and Bohol.
4. The west side of the Philippine triangle is made up of a long narrow
island, Palawan, and a cluster called the Calamian islands.
The total land area of the Philippines is about 114, 400 square miles. It
is thus larger than Italy or New Zealand, for example, and nearly as large as the
British Isles or Norway. Of the states in the Union, only Texus, California,
Montana and New Mexico excced it in size. The island of Luzon covers some 40, 814
square miles, Mindanao 36,906, Stmar 5,124, Negros 4,903, Palawan 4,500, Panay 4,448,
Mindoro 3, 794, and Leyte, 2, 799. In a1] there ure thirty islands more than 100

The Philipnine Islands are often spoken of as part of Malaysia. That is,

Malay or Malayun. Sumatra, Java, Bornco, the Culebes to the south of the Philip-

The climate of the islands is tropical. The southernmost parts of Mindanao
and Sulu are only about five degrees north of the equator. Yet there are some high
mountain ranges cool enough to have a touch of frost at night, and peaks soar here
and there to about ten thousand feet above sca level. But the coastal and lowland
areas are mainly hot and humid. The northern rogions of the Philippines are subject
to occasional destructivo typhoons, the "typhoon scason" being from June to Ootober.
The brown-skinned Filipinos are well accustomed to the torrid sun and the
rainy dampness, though to the person from tomperate zones they are enervating.
Dur ing the cooler winter from November to April tho west coast of Luzon on which the
capital city, Manila, is situated, has a drier season, whilo the east coast has rain.
Rain comes to the west coust in the hot season, May to October, during whioh time
the east is drier. This difference comes about through the chenges in the monsoon
winds, which, blowing from the northeast and southwest alternatively, meet the
central mountains and discharge thoir rain on the windward sides. The middle part
of the archipelugo, with its soattered islands, has a less marked seasonal change
from wet to dry. Some of the mountainous scctions of the interior catoh rain from
both monsoons and huve a heavy rainfall most of the year. Theso differences in
climato would bo of the greatest importance to anyono studying Filipino agriculture,
for the annual cyoles of planting and harvest vary from placc to place aocording to
how the seasons run.
Plant and animal lifc in the Philippines is abundant. Wherever men have

Negrito people still survive in the Philippines, mainly in the north of Luzon.
Later comers have pushed them back into the deeper forests und swamps, and they
number today perhaps thirty thousand.

CHAPTER V
VASSALS OF SPAIN
In 152l, the Spanish voyager Magellan rounded Cape Horn and, orossing the
Paoifio with his little fleet of caravels, reached the Philippines. We oannot
dwell here upon the story of European expansion into the "New World" of whioh this
was a part, nor of the rivalries between the Portuguese, Spanish, Dutoh, and later
the English and French for the rich trade of the spice islands and the Orient. By
this time, middle and south uncrica were part of the Spanish domain. It was from
there that the newly "discovered" Islas Filipinas were conquered and colonized.
Magellan lost his life when assisting in a faotion fight between warring
Filipino groups. Nearly a half century passod before Spain oooupied the islands
permanently. Then, in an almost unbolievably short timo, a small handful of
soldiers and priests led by a Spanish officer, Miguel Legaspi, acquired the sub-

by a youth twenty-two years old, Legaspi's grandson Juan de Salcedo, who with forty-
five men in his command ranged a large part of Luzon compelling the soattered
Filipino settlements to yield allegiance. It was this laok of wider political
unities that made the Spanish task so casy.
Legaspi soon cstablished his capital at the busy trading port of Manila.

quarter century of Legaspi's arrival, only the Mohammedans of the south and the

Yet Spain's hold on the Philippines was for many years precarious. Her

CHAPTER VI
THE SPANISH HERITAGE IN THE PHILIPPINES
The Filipinos of today still bear profoundly the marks of these centuries
of Spanish domination. To a person who did not know this phase of the island
history, muoh of Filipino life and charaoteristios would be incomprehensible.
Away in the future, historians will probably write that Spain's great
contribution to the Filipinos was one she had no intention of giving. The Spaniards
assumed control of a host of pctty independont groups. They left a unified nation
well on towards the making. In spite of their polioics of keeping the Indios in
peaceful subjoeotion, the inevitable fruits of thoir presonoc in the islands and of
their exactions was to weld the population more and more into a whole. By opening
up the country, they made the looal dwellers aware of one another. Oppression gave
a sense of common grievanoc. Discontent emerged into patriotic organization.
Pressure from above formed an inoreasingly solid native body politio beneath. It
is, of course, un old story in the process of human development - seemingly
nature's main way of nation building. But in the Philippines it is of special
signifioanoe because of the varieties of races and cultures that existed there.
Spain also contributed in a more positivc way cloments from hor own rich
heritage of the past. An educated or illustrado class emerged among the Filipinos,
ospeoially among the rich land-owning groups around Menila many of whom were of
part Spanish or Chinese blood. These learnt the Spanish language, and adopted the

Filipinos and took toll of American lives during the campaigns were inoreasingly
brought under oontrol. Freedom of religious worship was guaranteed. An arrangement
was made by which the lands of the friars were bought on behalf of the Philippine
government, and largely reallotted to Filipino farmers.
As would be cxpected, the commeroial development of the islands proceeded
apace. The finances of government were organized so that from the first the
Philippines have been practically self-supporting. From 1909 on, tariff laws were
framed through whioh Philippine goods came to have free entry to American markets
and vice versa. The cconomio life of the islands became closely woven with that of
the United States, and trade with other countries dwindled.
The great city of Manila, with its four hundred thousand residents, the
expanding industries of growing sugar, hemp, coconuts and other products suited to
suoh a tropical land, the extensive services of modern government, the rising
standards of life that are relieving the ordinary Filipino people more and more from
the drudgery and poverty of the Spanish period - all these have been built upon the
decision of the Imerican congruss and people to open the doors of trade. Today,
those doors are being closed. Many Americans are disolaiming responsibility for
the situation thus oreated: perhaps even advocating independence in order to

relieve themselves of competition from Philippine goods. The meaning of this for

CHAPTER VIII
A LAND OF CONTRASTS
The Philippine Islands today are indeed e place of contrasts. Our boat

must be orossed, that is, the more or less empty belt of country between the

fields, bringing back their wages in the form of bundles of salt, carabaos, pigs,
dogs, or metal to convert into tools and weupons.
The forest folk whom we enoounter may live in tiny groups of three or four
houses beside a stream. The men hunt and fish, while the women grow dry rioe, beans
and other produce in clearings out from season to season in the dense jungle! Food
comes plentifully in normal times. Their main struggle seems to be with the dread
malaria that keeps the population soanty. Knowing practically nothing as yet of
modern medicine, they seek to appease the malignant spirits of disease by sacrificing
dogs and by making other offurings. Deeper yet in the jungle, may be encountered
people who havo their huts high up in the forks of trees. We may even come upon
some band of timid pygmies, roving with their bows and arrows, though it is unlikely
that we will actually see these folk unless we have a guide who is on friendly terms
with them.
Going higher and higher, we come at last abovo the jungle belt. Groat
peaks and ranges soar up perhaps to nearly ten thousand foet above sea level. Thoy
are scarred by gorges and river courses. The gentler slopes are clothed with pine
and oak trees and even grassy moadows. In this isolated and not altogether hospita-
ble region live perhaps the most hardworking of Filipinos, the pagan "highlanders."
Through the centuries they have built stupondous terraces fed by elaborate irrigation

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
-50-
systems, in which to grow rice and other foods upon the mountain sides. All life
for them is bound around the annual cycle of agrioulture. Women work in the fields
along with the men. The gods and ancestors, too, are invoked with sacrifices of
pigs, chiokens and other valued goods to do their part in warding off the starvatio
that comes with a bad season. Almost every village was formerly a self-contained
unit, hostile to all others. Its thousand or so people have intermarried closely
through the centuries, and each group may have many customs which even neighbors do
not share. But today as we have noted, conditions are changing. The people,
especially the young folk, are conmencing to mingle among themselves and with the
Christian lowlanders. They are Filipinos in the making, even though it may take a
long time to assimilate them to the general life of the country.
If, instead of striking inland, we had sailed to the southern coasts, we
would have come to Moro country. The Mohammedans are essentially a people who live
by the water. Some have their houses on piles over the sea and all are at home in
the slender vintas that once engaged in pirate raids. Their clothing is elaborately
ornamented. Gay red and yellow colors predominate. Those who can afford it have
more than one wife, as the Koran allows this. The folk of each district look to
their own dato or petty sultan. Until recently the Moros were linked more olosely
with other Mohammedans noross the straits in Borneo than with the Christian
Filipinos. But a sense of being Filipino is now emerging, and as with the pagans
hostility toward the Christian is slowly but surely passing.
So much for a first glance at the Philippines today. It is now our task
to examine more systematically the contemporary scene, espccially the problems that
face the Filipino people now that their national independonce is in sight.

CHAPTER TX
CROWDS AND EMPTY SPACES
The natural starting point for our analysis is to know something about the
basic facts of population
The last census of the Philippines, taken in 1918, showed a total of
nearly ten and a half million Filipinos. By 1934, it was estimated that there had
been an inoroase to thirteen millions, or more than the combined populations of
Norway, Sweden and Denmark, and about one fifth that of Japan. A recent count of
the non-Filipino residents showed about 71,000 Chinose, 19,500 Japanese, 7,700
Amerioans, 5,500 Spaniards, and 3,000 of other nationalities.
According to truso ficures, the Philippines have an uverage of about 114
people to the squar. mile, In itself, this does not tell us much. If they were
barren and mountainous it might well represent a cupucity population. On the other
hand, we have secn the islands to be for tho main part rich and fertile. Compared
with somewhat similar aruas in many countries in asia and Europe they are undor-
populated. A Filipino scholar estimates that on the present busis of economio life,
the Philippines could support fifty million people comfortably, and if intensive
agriculture and lower st: ndards of living prevailed they could hold ninety to a
hundred million, that is, nearly one quarter the population of China.1 The
Philippines aro thus an undersettled and underdeveloped country. a land of pioneer
regions. The real import of this is appuront if we recall that only a short
distance away are coastal China and islund Japun, which are among the most densely
populated regions of the carth.

Skotch map 3 shows the distribution of the Filipinos over their country
today. From it appears a further striking point. The coasts and river val leys are
thickly settlcd, also to quito an cxtont the acccssible plains. But vast areas of
hinterland are sparsely populated or empty. A part of this land is, of course,
mountainous und unfit for sottlement cxcept as the hill folk have worked out means
to eke a living from it. Yet the Bureau of Forestry has calculated that nearly
47,0o0 square mil, s, or wcll over one third of the total area of the Philippine
Islands, consists of cultivuble lunds yet unoultivuted. The great island of Mindanao
is particularly emoty, in contrast to much of Luzon which is thickly settled.
This concentration of the Filipinos in sp cial places can casily be under-
stood in the light of history and of their manners of life. We suw how the majority
of the early migrants were soafaring people, Indonesians and Malayans, who settled
along the coast or on the rivers and stroums. Fishing was one of their main ocoupa-
tions. The kin groups wo ld live close togother not only for defensive purposes but
also because of the close family and social bonds characteristic of Filipino life.
At an carlier point, rcferonce was mado to the great increase in population
that has takon place in modern days. Now this came about among the lowland coastal
dwellers rather than the isolated non-Christians. In tho island of Panay, for
example, the Spaniurds collected 22, 000 tributes in 1586, which means that the
population probably nurbered 90,000. In 1928 it was ustimated that Panay conteinod
over one million inhabitants. Since the Filipino likus to stay among his kinsmon
the anocstral living site, und only exccptional necessity or ambition causos him to
leave, this hes meant that the forner small barangays have become large, crowded
barrios. In the Filipino family, young marricd folk live either in the home of one
or other parents, where they form part of the essential work force of the household,
or else build a home near by.

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
-53-
But there is only a limited amount of agricultural land available around
eaoh town or village. Much of that is now usually owned by a few rich cacique
families (page 31). This means that the people have to work their own little
holdings more intensively to feed the ever increasing number of mouths. Or else
they have to become share tenants or laborers under praotioally starvation conditions
for their more favorably situated fellows. The only alternatives are for them, if
they oan, and if they are willing to break the home ties, either to go away for
longer or shorter periods to places where they oan earn money, or else to pioneer
new homesteads in frontier regions.
Here we are getting down to some of the basic problems of Filipino life.
Such economic pressuro is the main reason why so many thousands of younger Filipinos
have oome to the United Statcs, working as plantation and garden laborers, musicians,
waiters and the like. They want to earn money. The great majority of them are
sending money home to help pay the family debts, or if they are more fortunate to
buy land that will onable them to beoome independent and so perhaps graduate to the
aacique class.
In the Philinpines itself, there is a seasonal ebb and flow of men and to
some extent women to industrial and urban centers where labor for wages is available.
If it were not for the excellent rouds that now form a network over the Philippines,
and the oommeroial development that now is threatened through partial closing of the
American markets, the poorer Filipinos would be more and more at the meroy of the
landlords. Many would be faced by the alternatives of starvation or near slavery
unless thoy moved to new pluces.
Why, it may be asked, have the peoplo in the crowded areas not moved to
settle the empty areas? Many, of course, are accustomed to serving the landlords
willingly, for their anoestors have done so right back to early times. The churoh
has taught that humility and resignation are virtues. Short of being made dissatis-

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
-54-
fied by the slow infiltration of American economio and educational ideas, they know
of nothing better. They depend on the cacique not only to organize their agri-
oulture but also, perhaps, to recommend wives for their boys and girls and advise
them on other intimate matters of their lives. Until nowadays neither they nor
their forefathers have dreamt of a society where every man stands on his own feet.
The ordinary man in the Philippines is often called "Juan de la Cruz"
just as in America he is sometimes labelled "John Doe," or in England "John Bull."
Now, say Juan de lu Cruz gets un idea that he and his family should move away and
settle on new lands. Just becuuse ho is in dire need or ho would not reach the
extreme decision of leaving the ancestral barrio, he is suro to be in debt to the
landlord. But it costs money to move. He must have rail, bus or boat fare; or
even if he loads his goods and family on to a carabao cart how will he feed himself
in the months required to break in an area of unoccupied jungle and grow his first
crops? He cannot go to the landlord to borrow more money or rice. The landlord
wants him to stay and work, and indeed is almost sure to start legal proceedings to
collect the outstanding debts that will land the ambitious Juan in jail.
Added to these difficulties, Juan fears the lonely places. In his own
bamboo house safe in the village he shuts the windows at night to keep out the
wandering anitos (spirits) und perhaps at certain times puts out offerings of food
to please them. To go to the end of the trails and face the lush jungle with its
unknown ghostly terrors, to livo and work day after day awey from his sociable
fellows, requires a nerve that his medieval training has hardly fostered. And his
fears are not all imaginings. In newly cut jungle country, the dread malaria is at
its worst, and if he himself survives it is practically sure to take toll among his
family. A poem has been written giving some idou of the experiences of those who
have ventured forth in spite of all theso handioaps:

There are added complications to this pionecring. The land laws of tho
Philippines are yet very cumbersome. It may be yoars before Juan can get a paper
from the government indicating that the new homestead is his own. Cases are by no
means unknown where unscrupulous people who have a knowledge of the law trick
settlers out of their holdings so laboriously broken in. Again, these uninhabited
places are often the ancestral hunting grounds of tho non-Christian forest and
mountain people. These rosent the onorouchment of the Christian lowlanders upon
their traditional territories. The government is having a delioate task to keep
suoh newly pacified folk from doscending upon the settlers with their head-axes, us
has happened on more than one occusion.
It is now noro easy to see why tho Filipino population is spread so un-
evenly. Today, many American und Filipino lcuders consider this the outstanding
internal problem of the country. The governmont has launohed sevcral colonization
sohemes by which familics from crowded places are trunsported in compact groups to

new areas, given the necessary finanoial support to aoquire land and establish them-

Two of the many groups among the Philippine population are rather

The Ilocanos are often spoken of as tho Scotohmen or Jews of the islands.
Their lands are densely settled, and tho villagers are hardworking and thrifty as
a result of their efforts to win a living from the narrow strip of soil that lies
between the soa and the high mountains. Poople who say that Filipinos are indolent
and unenterprising always mako an exception of these Ilocanos. In recent decades,
thousands of Ilocano individuals and families have migrated to the wide-spreading
plains between their country and Manila, likewise to a large river basin called the
Cagayan valley in northernmost Luzon where they were first taken to grow tobacco for
Spanish commercial interests. Tho majority of Filipinos who have come to the

they have tonded to oust and absorb them. Of this, more will be said shortly.

CHAPTER X
UNITY OUT OF DIVERSITY
We have indiouted that there are local groups in the Philippines with
many varieties of speech and custom: heritage of the complex peopling of the
islands. The task of this seotion is to see more clearly what these differenox are,
and how far in the modern process of nation building there has come about a greater
unity.
It is not casy to got from tho books that have beon written on the
Filipinos a clear ideu of the likenesses and difforences emong thom. Some writers
have stressed tho variations from district to listriot. They make much of the
contrast between the Christians and the non-Christians, and give an impression thut
the Filipinos cannot bocome a unified people exoept, perhaps, aftor a very long
time indeed. Others take an opposite viowpoint. They say that, except perhaps for
the tiny handful of pygmy folk, the Filipinos are a kindred Indo-Malayan people,
basically one, with local differenoes that are boooming no more important, say, than
those in the United States between city and country, north and south, New York and
California, descendunts of Filgrim Fathers and of recont immigrants from Europe.
Filipino leaders incline strongly to the latter view as part of their assertion that
they are ready for indopendence. Thero does the truth lie?
As tho Spaniards brought one after anothor of the independent little
communities under control in the sixteenth contury, they noted that they could be
olassed on the basis of language and custom into larger wholes. The people in and
around Manila, for example, spoke more or less the sane tongue, worshipped the sam
deities, and allowing for slight differenocs fron settlement to settlement were
essentially similar folk. That they fought anong thomselves a great deal, perhaps,
did not make them culturally difforent; this happened also among the anoestors of

A few comments will make these divisions clearer. In the first place,
they represent important differenoes of speech. Scholars are able to trace all the
Philippine languages to a common ancestry. Also it is rather easier for a Filipino
from one area to learn another dialect than for, say, an Englishman to learn Dutch
or German, or a Frenchman to learn Italian. The dialects have an akin grammatical
structure and other elements in common. Yet for immediate praotical purposes only
the people of each local group can understand one another in their native dialect.
To a Tagalog, for instance, the talk of an Ilocano or Visayan is incomprehensible,
unless exceptional circumstanoes have caused him to learn that form of speech.
Indeed, sometimes within the same group there are differences from district to
distriot that make understanding diffioult, as among the Visayan dialects. The
Philippine scene has thus been one of great linguistio diversity.
Much the sume applies to customs, beliefs, ideas of right and wrong, and
the like. There hus been an essential sameness underlying the ways of nearly all
Filipino units. Family and social life follow the same general principles of
organization, the older religious ideas were broadly alike, and so on. But from
group to group there developed important differences in detail that clearly marked
each from the others. And it is these detailed things, such as what at any parti-

oular time, it is "correct" to eat, wear, do or leave undone, laugh at, or believe
in, that determine to a great extent whether people are friendly and respeotful to

It is no part of our task to spe oulato how all these local specialisms
might have come about as a result of group isolation or contact. Rather we want to
know how far they are important in modern days. Do they forn a barrier to Filipino
unity and cooperation? Aro large groups prevailing over small groups, and is one
type of spoech and oustom omerging to replace all the others?
Let us pioture the results of modern neuce and intercourse. As peoples
hitherto isolated bogan to travel in one unother's districts, exchanging ideas,
trading, and perhaps marrying and settling, they had to make adjustments in their
traditional ways. The border-lines between ethnic units tended to become blurred.
The growing cities and towns had mixed populations. Vihere one group seemed to have
easier methods of doing things, or better goods und ideas, the others might oopy
them. One essential necd was to have somo meuns of common communication.
Three of the local dialects cmerged in modern times as most important for
suoh intercourse. Tagulog, the spoech of the Manila people, who were in closest
touoh with the whites, was leurnt by all the other folk in the central Philippines.
In the north, Ilocano migrunts (puges 55-56) carriod their dialect with them. Even
the pagan groups of tho northorn jungles and mountains learnt to use Ilocano as a
medium for common contact among one another, and as the language of trading and
government activities which brought contact with the outside world. Among the
southern Philippine groups, Visuyan spread likewise, especially the dialeot of Cabu.
It was a great natural cxperiment in self-education.
Today, it would be a very rare Filipino who cannot speak one of theso
three major dialects. it the sume time, however, those who do not count them as
the mother tongue preserve their own particular dialects for use in the family

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
-62-
and barrio. Furthermore, few Filipinos can speak more than one of these major
dialects. Even among the Filipinos who have come to the United States, the Tagalog
speaking persons cannot understand those speaking the Ilocano or Visayan, nor the
latter one another. No one dialect has become a national tongue. For common
interoourse, those who are able have to use Spanish or English, and otherwise an
interpreter must be called in.
Some observers see in this lack of a generally understood speech a serious
bar to national unity. It is one reason why American educators have been pushing
hard the learning of English. among certain ardent Filipino nationalists, a move-
ment has started to maku, not En:lish, but one of the three main Philippine
languages thcir common tongue. But unfortumately the Tagalogs insist that it should
be Tagalog, while Ilocanos and Visayans think that their home dialects are the best.
Meantime educated Filipinos curry on the national business variously in Spanish and
English, with English steadily coming to predominate. Their uneducated countrymen
can at best speak in the common dialect of the locality. The Philippine soene is
thus linguistically very complicated. Ultimately, many believe, English will be
the wider intellectual language of the Filipinos, with probably a fused Malayan
dialect based on Tugalog as a unoondary tongue; but that day is yot far off.
Language is a vehiclo by which the ideus and values of a community carry
over from person to person and goneration to generation. Along with the dialects,
therefore, many of the local customs have been presorved. The Filipino of today,
even the member of a small minority group, cherishes the ancestral ways. At the
same time he knows the ways of his neighbors to quito an extent, and may have
incorporated some of them voluntarily into his life. Where, for cxample, the pagans
of the hills formorly took one crop of rice from their fields each year, many of
them are now imitating the low) nders and cultiv tin: two crops.
Sometimes, however, mcmbers of one group ussume an attitude of superiorit.

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
-63-
over others, and this is reciprocated; then there is trouble and ill-feeling. The
non-Christian often feels that the Christians look down on him and his ways with
pity if not contempt, so he tends to keep apart. The Manila folk are accused of
holding themselves above the rural groups. But then the same is said of New Yorker
or Bostonians. Undoubtedly the trend is for sectionalism to be tempered more and
more by a wider sense of being Filipino.
Among the most important influences in creating a groater unity have been
trade, religion, political organization, and education. These will be dealt with
more fully as the study prooeeds. Here, we can remind ourselves that, at least
among the majority of Filipinos, the local ways became overlaid in days past by a
common heritage of Spanish-Christiun ideas and customs. (Chapter VI). Likewise the
life of the Moro groups was reorganized in relation to the Mohammedan faith. Today,
roads, schools, government offices, stores, newspapers, radios, and the like are
being effeotive in linking the people together more and more as a whole. While the
Spaniards were inclined to look askanoe at any movement towards Filipino unity, the
Americans are cooperating heartily with Filipino leaders in trying to enoourage it.
Especially in the case of the backward peoples, a steady pressure is being exerted
toward "assimilation."
The modern scene, therefore, is one in which the old localisms of speech,
custom and loyelty are to a lcrge oxtent preserved, yet there is emerging over and
above these a common national life und loyalty. On the ono hand, there is great
conservatism. Many Filipinos still know little, or are interested in little outside
the immediate round of family and community life. On the other, the people as a
whole are being introduced as fast us circumstances allow to all from western
civilization that is considered of benefit in nation building, and a spirit of
Filipino citizenship is being fostered.
It is not casy, as mattors stand today, to pronounoe judgment as to how far

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
-64-
the Filipinos can be oalled a unified people. There is a danger into which some
observers have fallen, of confusing unity with uniformity. In our own western
nations we do not demand that everyone follow exactly the same patterns of life in
order to be good citizens. Pride in our city, state, and special ways of doing
things are by no means inconsistent with loyalty to the nation. In the case of the
Filipinos, too, it would be false to say that the people cannot be one so long as
all these local differences exist. Indeed, rooted as these are in the soil and the
history of each region, they form a rich heritage uny undue destruction of which
would be a distinct cultural loss to the Filipino nution. We should therefore
suspend judgment on this question of unity until the present day life of the
Filipino people has been reviewed more fully.

CHAPTER XI
THE WORKER OF THE SOIL
According to the census of 1918, seventy-two per cent of Filipino men were
agrioulturalists. Had the census been adjusted to local oonditions, it would have
been recorded, too, that the majority of women worked in the fields, and even the
boys and girls in the rural families took their part in the seasonal oyole of
oultivation.
The department of agriculture and commerce of the Philippine government
estimates that in 1932 sugar was produced in the islands to the value of nearly
60 million dollars,1 rice worth nearly 45 millions, coconuts and copra 16 millions,
corn 5 millions, hemp 4 millions and tobacco 32 millions. Many other agricultural
produots bring the total valuo to about 150 million dollars. Almost the whole of
Philippine exports are made up of agrioultural conmodities, notably sugar, copra
and coconut oil, hemp and tobacoo.2 In other words, the economio and commeroial
life of the islands is built up direotly upon the labor of those who work the soil.
Under American wardship, the way has been opened for economio betterment.
Life has become more stable and seoure. People can accumulate property without
fear of confiscation. Trade is carried on from district to distriot, and with the
larger world outside. Where, before, Juan de la Cruz and his family had to make
for themselves praotically overything they needed, - tools, olothing, pottery and
so forth - now they can buy it from the barrio storekoeper or the oity shops,
provided they have the money. Likewise they can concentrate their labor upon

speoial tasks the products of which are in demand: agriculture, for instanoe, or

the possibilities of better material standards of living.
Nevertheless, scholars tell, the Filipinos as a people are still very poor,
"so poor that they can hardly make both ends meet."3 It is estimated that their
average per capita wealth is $233, as compared with $380 for Japan and $2,918 for the
United States. Even this wealth is more oonoentrated into the hands of a few than
in our own country. A Filipino leader estimates that the average property of the
tenant olass is worth only about $10.4 Professor Macaraig, of the University of the
Philippines writes:
"The earnings, or shares, of the tenants in the produots of their
work are so small that they oan hardly subsist on them. They are not
properly olothed, and they can not buy shoes... They do not eat nourish-

When their relatives die, they must depend upon their neighbors' contri-

the rice orop, they have no savings upon which to draw. When their poorly

and depend upon relatives and friends for support."
Why are the vast majority of Filipinos so poor? The writer just quoted
gives five rousons as follows. (a) The natural rosources of the Philippines are not
yet well developed. (b) The rich folk exploit the poor. (o) The Filipinos love
gambling. and this tends to make them lazy and to keep them in poverty. (d) "Becaus.
See Serafin E. Macaraig, Community Problems, chapters I, IV, X, XVIII, XIX.
4
President Camilo Osias of tho National University of the Philippines, quoted by
Macaraig, op.oit., p. 179.
5
Nipa is a palm, the leaves of which are used for thatching.

we do not have many necessities in life, we do not work hard enough to acoumulate
wealth. We have no cold winter to worry about. We need no coal or expensive
woolen clothing. We can live in nipa houses and on rice and fish without starving.
The result is that our people become lazy. They depend on what they get from nature
in an easy manner. They do not work hard to produce wealth, and so our country is
poor." (e) Filipinos do not practice thrift and exeroise foresight. "When they
have plenty of money, they spend it carelessly on fiestas and expensive clothing,
or throw it away in gambling. They do not...look ahead and save their money for
a carabao cart, but spend at the rate of an eight cylinder car."
It is worth looking more closely at reason - (d) above. We must always
be careful about measuring the ways of living of other peoples by our own. What we
think is good or bad, wealth or poverty, and so on does not always correspond to
their ideas: each folk has its own standards of value. We may, for instance,
believe an Australian Aborigine to be very badly off on his parched deserts living
on whatever kangaroos and other game he can catch. But he certainly would not
thank us if we took him into a Park Avenue mansion. To him it would be a suffocat-
ing prison, and the foods, crockery and cutlery on its spotless tablecloth quite
"foreign" and unaprealing: he would long for his open hunting grounds and the joys
of life in a country where so-oalled civilized men, for all their knowledge, woul
quiokly starve. To many a Filipino peasant, ten or twenty dollars would represent

What of the peasant of the crowded regions, where onportunity is much

picture has also written the story of a tenant in Nueva Ecija, one of the

"The Nucva Eciju peasant," he says, "knows that he is a peasant -

in Nueva Ecija. But huro he was born, and here he must stuy, for he has no

He lives with his family of five or six in a small house on a corner
of the piece of land he cultivates - big or small as it pleasod the Hacendero
(landlord or cacique) to upportion him. No matter how industrious and thrifty
he may be, he can not hope to ever own the land he labors on, nor any other
piece of land in the province, for in Nuevu Eciju and other Central Luzon
provinces, the agrioultural lands are owned by a fuw rich hacenderos, and no
tenants' money can buy suoh land.
He usually hires his work animals from the Hacendero. He keeps them
in a small enolosure at night, and gets up at four or five o'clook in the morn-

. If a tenant borrows a sum of money

There is no other
way: he borrows from the Hacendero at the same usurious rate of interest. And so

The tenant feels that something should be done about it; he thinks the

He gous to othor tenants and they discuss the possibility of forming

nd if they escaped the ever-watchful Constabulary agents, there

This grim picture of tenant life represents the other oxtreme from the
Cagayan soene. The reasons that have made it possible were analysed earlier

for those times, in which chief and commoner each had privileges and responsibili-

"The cacique," an American has remarked, "is a cross between an English
country gentlemen, an American ward politiciap,and a Spanish grandee." Numbers of
landlords must be given the credit of being fine men, interested in their country

grasping and there is a surplus of agricultural workers.
It is not hard for us to see why, in the modern Philippines, there is a
growing agrarian discontent:
"For the last twenty years there have been many conflicts between the
Kasama (Tagalog word for tenant) and the cacique in the Philippines.
"Today the Kasama is better oducated. He knows his rights and
privileges. He knows when his landlord is exploiting him. The Kasama
wants better food and clothing and a strong-material house instead of the
nipa shack. He wears shoes and is proud to have a phonograph. In other
words, his wants and necessities have inoreased, but his earning capacity
has remained the same. To be able to supply his increased needs, the
Kasama is asking the cacique to give him a larger share of the crop. He
is asking also a lower rate of interest on the money he borrows. The
cacique will not give what the Kasama is asking for. This is the funda-
mental cause of the conflict between the capitalist and the laborers on
the farms of the Philippines."4
When we road in the papers about agrurian troubles in the islands, we oan
be quite sure that here lies the fundamental cause. It also enables us to see why
those younger people who can get away, leave for the cities or come abroad.
The cacique system, it is further said, is a drawback to the development

CHAPTER XIII
COMMERCE AND INDUSTRY
In 1899, the total trade of the Philippines was valued at about
$34,000,000. In 1928, it had risen to near1y $290,000,000, and in 1933 stood at
180,500,000. Sugar has been easily the principal export, with coconut products,
abaca (hemp), tobaoco,embroideries, and lumber as the main sccondary exports. In
turn, the Philipnines buy cotton goods, iron and steel products, mineral oils, meat
and dairy products, automobiles, paper, wheat flour, and a great varioty of other
articles.
Where at the leginning of the American regime more than 80 per cent of
Philippine exports went to Europe and Asia and only 13 per cent to the United States,
in 1933 over 86 per cent went to the United States. During recent years, about
three quarters of all Philippine trade has been conducted with her guardian. The
primary factor in this was the establishment of reciprooal free trade relations
since 1909 (page 39). This move wes at first opposed by Filipino leaders, as they
feared that if Philippine cconomic life were too closcly cntangled with that of
America it would be impossible to secure political frecdom. Nevertheless, the
United States took upon hersulf the responsibility of developing Philippine comu erse
and industry in this way. By now, the welfarc o. many thousands of Filipinos in the
cities and industrialized districts, and the finunoes of government, havo become
almost inextricably bound up with the uxistenoe of a market for Philippine goods in
Amerioa. The meening of this for the independence issue, and the offects of tariff
and quota restrictions now being inposed by Congress will be disoussed later.
Under American guidance, an uctive department of agriculture and commeroc
has been built up within the Philippine government. Bureaus are established that
deal with the weather, navigation, soientifio research, lunds, forestry, mineruls,

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.99)
-81-
fish and game resources, plant industry, animal industry, home economios, labor,
publio works, trade promotion and regulation, and cooperative marketing and credits.
A large staff of scientists, travelling inspectors and instruotors, engineers and
the like, now comprised almost wholly of trained Filipinos, are at work.
In the early years of the American regime, certain fundamental problems
of economic welfare were concentrated upon. Plant and animal diseases were brought
under control  s a result of scientific researoh. Experimental stations were set
up at various points to i prove the quality of ugricultural produots and of
domestio animals, to introduce new and profitablc crops, and to demonstrate machine
methods, the use of fertilizers, and the most effective ways of preparing products
for the market. A colloge of agriculture was established as part of the University
of the Philippines. Farm truining was given in the sohools. Laws were made that
would settle the ownership of lands, and conserve and develop the rich forests and
other resources of the islands. People now knew when to expect typhoons or other
bad storms, and so could batten down their houses and save their property. The
coasts were mapped, and lighthouses placed at dangerous points.
Wherever these new measures touched the traditional life of the people at
any vital spot, the conservatism alrcady spoken of became manifest, at least in the
early years. In one district, the rosidents atiacked government workers who were
sent to dig water-wells, becauso of an old belief that "any tampering with the
shell of the earth would offend the spirits." Where diseased carabaos or other
stock had to be killed, their owners resented such action. People were slow to see
why they should have to pay to get papers from the government to show thoir title
to lands that came down according to the customary modes of inheritance from their
forefathers, or why they should not be able to cut timber freely in the forests.
And so it has been in phase after phase of modern economic and social change. Only
by a process of educetion or clear demonstration that benefits were forthcoming,

=== Page 226 === (confidence 0.99)
-82-
havo tho government activities been made effective.
Suoh conservatism tends to be manifested among people the world over when
an established order of things is being threatened by innovations. Nevertheless we
can well realize why Juan de la Cruz is particularly suspicious of ideas introduced
by strangers, whether American or Filipino, when we look back to the oppressions
and exaotions of Spanish days. He is learning only slowly that a government can be
disinterested, thot promisos are not made to be broken, and that there are people
outside his immediute kin who may be trusted and can have his intereste sincerely at
heart. Meantimo he tends to prefer things he knows, even if almost unendurably bad,
to unknown things backed only by promises of better times to come.
One of the first ccts of the American authorities was to form a Philippine
bureau of labor. Tho growth of largo commeroial and industrial enterprises such as
sugar centrals, lumber mills, tobucoo factories, und the business of transportation
by land and sea opened up thousands of wago-earning jobs in the islands. The
bureau has served to protect the workers from exploitation by employers or by un-
scrupulous labor leaders, regulating conditions of work, mediating in disputes,
inspecting plantations and factories, and supervising the emigration of laborers to
Hawaii. Unfortunatoly the industrial enterprises are usually in districts far from
the crowded agricultural provinces. It is therefore impossible for many of those
most in need of wago-carning jobs to move thither because of transportation costs.
Nevertheless there is an incrcasing movement of seasonal labor in the islands from
centors of unemployment to rugions where agricultural and industrial work for wages
is available, involving perhaps several hundrod thousand people annually.
A tradition has grown up in the islands that the Filipino is not a good
wage-laborer. Thus some sugar planters and other omployers of labor have claimed
that Filipinos are unroliuble. Many, including Filipino capitalists, have urged
from time to time that Chinese laborers bo introduced to meet their needs.

=== Page 227 === (confidence 0.99)

CHAPTER XIV
WHAT OF SELF-GOVERNMENT?
The United States, wo have seen ( chapter VII), sought to organize
among the Filipinos a stable, demooratic government, as a preparation for future
nationhood. Again and again, American leaders have stated that "the Philippines
are for the Filipinos." Independence was promised as soon us the people showed
themselves capable of maintaining a responsible and efficient administration.
For years, now, Filipino spokesmen have insisted that this requirement
has been fully met.
"Missions" have visited Washington to press for "immediate,
complete and absolute independence"; assemblies and demonstrations have been
organized in the islands; and numerous writers and orators have deolared with
fervor that "independence is the only way to national self-realization," and
that "only thus oan the four centuries of struggle and privation in the interests
of liberty come to fruition." Some have even claimed that "the present laok
of freedom prevents any movement for social and economic reform or self-oriticism
among the Filipino people."
Americans familiar with the Philippines have been divided in their
opinions. A certain number have declared that the Filipino leaders are fully
capable of taking the administration of affairs into their hands.
Some have
said that the United States oan do no moro toward teaching them, and they must
learn the rest by trying themselvos out; any delay means further distrust and
animosity toward Amorica. Others, inoluding various investigators sent from
Washington during tho post-war Republioan regime, have denied that the conditions
originally luid down were near fulfilment. For a long time to come, they say,
independenoe would meroly introduce rule by an oligarohy of the few educated and
rich folk.
They also assert that economio and political developments in the

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
-90~
larger world are such that launohing a small people upon an independent life
would be to their detriment.
Congress, by passing the Philippine Independence (Tydings-McDuffie) Aot

after a period of trial and transition. The Filipino legislature has accepted
its terms, subject to possible revision that will give them greater economic
security.
 Short of some unforeseen development in American-Filipino relationship,

Besides this central organization of government, the Americans built up

have been an excellent training ground for Filipino leaders. The departments of
justice, commerce, public works, health, education and the like with headquarters in
Manila also have representatives in the various districts.
Therc are two "chartered cities" in the Philiprines, Manila and Baguio.

control their own affairs. The Christian provinces are divided into nearly nine
hundred "municipalities," with clected presidents and councillors to care for the

Nations are thought of as advanced or backward according to the physical,

Health
A mujor task th t tho merican administrators set themselves was to

Researoh wes conducted into the causes and curo of tropical diseases, with wonder-
ful results. Efforts were made not only to enforce laws relating to sanitation and
health, but also to convert the peo lo to modern m dical ideas so as to make the
laws really effective. Hygiene has been from the first a muin subject in the
schools.
By now, smallpox und bubonic plague, scourgos of the Spanish period, are
almost unknown. Cholera is under completo control. Malaria has decreased by
perhaps fifty per cent. The educated peo le are thoroughly alive to hygienic ideas.

Law and Justice
Juan de la Cruz has some right to be rather puzzled today as to how he
should or should not behave. Upon the old Indo-Malayan customs have been super-
imposed first a set of Spanish laws and legal  rocedures and then a set that are a
result of .merican influenoes.
Much of the barrio life of the Philippines is still governed by the old
"customary law" - the unwritten but cherished ways of marrying, inheriting property,
adoption, behaving towards relutives, and the like. These ure usually interpreted
and enforced by the community Iders and leaders. In our own merican society we
have many such traditional customs. No policeman is needod to get us to eat turkey
at Thanksgiving, or to comicl us to shako hands on meeting friends. The Filipino
practices connected with dobts and tenantry are rooted in the customary law. Where
anyone infringes such hoary regulations, he or she comes under a ban of public
disapproval or may cven be thought due for punishment by the spirits. Unluss,
therefore, a person is pr pared to leave tho community, it is to his advantage and
comfort to obey.
These rules of the family and burrio have had one fatal weakness. They
varied more or less widcly from groun to group. Where in old times a dispute trose
between two comnunitics, th. usual vay of showing whose ideas were more correct was
to resort to war.
The Spaniards, in ccifying and unifying the Philippines, had to curb t
absolute rowers of the local leaders and establish courts where any disputes that
in old days would have brought vwar could be settled. They went further, and ii.. oron
on the Filipinos a number of now laws which were folt to bo for the benefit of the
country or necessary for their government. To enforce these, they used soldiers,
civil guards, and prisons. Sometimes suoh laws run lirectly counter to the Indo-
Malayan customs. Thus the old practice at marriage w s for the family of a bride-

groom to give presents to that of a bride. The Spanish statute law, following

The Philippine islands have a very efficient prison system. This in-

Eduoation
Again and again in our Philippine story, the solution of problems appears
to await the progress of education. A basic feature of Amerioan policy has been
to give to the Filipinos free, seoular sohooling so fur as the resources of the
country have allowed. Where in 1907 there were hardly more than 6,000 pupils in
the public schools, today there are about 1,180,000, with a further 95,000 in
private institutions.
In Spanish times only the richer families could get their children
trained in the religious oolleges of Manila, and the ordinary people were fortunate
if they received oven a smattering of the "three Rs" in their local dialeots. By
the organic act of the Philippines (19o2), English was made the language of instruct-
ion in the newly formed Amorican schools. A new mental world commenoed to open up
before the Filipinos. Grcat emphasis was laid upon citizenship, health, agricul-
ture and sport. Dean Francisco Benitez, of the College of Blucation, University
of the Philippines, writes:
"American sovereignty has changed the aim of our education from the
narrow and religious one to a practical and dcmocratic aim. We have an educa-
tional system that relegates Bind loyalty, obedience and conformity, to a secondar.
position and stresses the cultivation of individual initiative, self-direction,
self control and the exercise of liberty under the law.
We have developed, under
American guidance, an educational system that aims to prepare for the present life
in this modern world." 2

CHAPTER XVI
THE CREATIVE SPIRIT
An interesting measure of the vitality and progress of any people is
their art and literature. These reveal their minds, emotions and philosophy of
life. In this section we shall see how far the Filipinos have been developing
such arts as nusic, danoing, puinting, und soulpture, likewise a literature of
their own. we shall a so sec how far these have grown out of the older back-
grounds, or represent an imitation and emulation of the West.
The pagan folk, whose life today gives somc clue to the old time condi-
tions, take great pleasure in chanting and in dancing to the beat of drums and
brass gongs. Some use flutes and more primitivo forms of string instruments.
Many of the pagans do fine wood-carving, woaving and motal-working; but here art
combines with utility, as the carved object may be a spoon, the ornamented cloth a
garment, and the shaped metal a weapon or pipe. Their literature is not written,
but is passed on from generation to generation by word of mouth. Around the fires
at night, or on oeremonial oocasions, the old men, or persons famed as story-
tellers relate tales of gods and heroes that come from ancient times. Here is an
abbreviation of such a tale of the god Kabigat, to whom a people called the Lepanto
pray for good luck.
"Long ago, there were two brother gods, Kabigat and Suyan. They
quarreled over a pig. Their father wanted to make peace between them but Suyan
would not agree. So at last the father made a shield, a spear and a bolo (knife)
for each of them. To decide who was at fault, he ordered them to fight each
other. For long they battled. At last Suyan was overwhelmed. That is why we
pray to Kabigat. When therc is a quarrel and a test is being made to see who is
in the right, we ask Kubigat to help the innocent purty."
A favorite type of tale is one with a moral, designed to make people obey

Some crude types of drama were also developed by the Filipinos under the
Spanish influence. The moro-moro show comprises boisterous theatricals portray-
ing the triumph of Christians over tho Mohammedan infidel. Two other kinds, the
duplo and thc Keragatun, have been described as follows:
"These are usually played at the celebration on the ninth day after a
death. The duplo is an elaborate dramatic debate in verse, usually ryhmed but
unsoanned, and without fixed stanzlic puttern...The players accuse one another
of mythical crimes in highly puzzling terms and the aocused dofend themselves in
torms just as puzzling...Witc clush und sparks fly about. Those whom the Muses
fail and are uneblo to ruad tho riddles propounded to them are either chastised
with a stick or made to say a long, elaborate prayer for the deceased...
"The karagatan... (is) somewhat less formal than the duplo for while
the dupleros are generally well-trained professionals, the players In a karagatan
are merely guests with roady purts and a turn for extemporanoous verification."
In view of such a mental and emotional atmosphere, in which the fresh currents of
only
modern knowledge have nly been blowing for a few years, can it be wondered that
the Filipinos tend to be highly romantic, and that many are labelled ignorant,
superstitious and credulous?

With the rise of nationalism toward the end of last century, the Filipino
spirit began at last to break free and express itself in artistry that was not mere
imitation, or religious and sentimentul medievalism. Dr. Rizal was, among his many
accomplishments, a peinter, soulptor, and writer. Other Filipinos who went abroud
studied in the art schools of Europe. The greatest of those was the painter, Lune;
other artists of merit were Hidalgo, de la Rosa and Amorsolo. Of soulptors,
Tolentino stands out as easily first. The works of all these men are a souroc of
pride to the cducated Filipinos of today, and a group of youngor artists are
following in their footstcps.
A lively literature hus also developed in the recent decades; first in
the dialects and Spenish, but now increasingly in English. Already Filipino writers
have been quoted at a number of points. Perhaps tho outstanding soholar the
Philippines have produced was Dr. Pardo do Tavera, who used Spanish. Besides having
a profound knowlcdge of Filipino lunguages and history, he wrote vigorously on
political and social problems. The revolutionary leaders such as Mabini and del
Pilar were nearly all cap ble writers. In fact it has been said that practioally
every Filipino leader of modern times has at one time or another turned his hand to
journalistic work. Where during the Spanish period such writings were done abroad
or in seoret, the years from l398 have seen a veritable flood of Filipino newspapers
and journals arise - though mostly to pass away as quickly. As we can expect, the
main theme has been the importance of achieving politioal freedom.
There has boen much discussion in reccnt yeurs as to whether the Filipino
can ever really cxpross his thoughts and emotions adequately in the "foreign"
English languagc. Certainly the bulk of published writings in that tongue have been
medioore. Even if the English is grummatically correct, it tends to be either a
self-consoious copying of west rn writers or else rather pompous and platitudinous.
Nevertheless certuin Filipinos now write English of outstanding merit. Furthermore,

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
-115-
a younger generation of authors are cmerging, trained in tho English-speaking
sohools, who seem well on the way to creating a special English-Filipino literature.
Now that the subjeot of politics tends to have lost its freshness, they are turning
to more directly artistic creation in both poetry and prose. Keeping the master-
picoes of English literature before them, they are going to their own country and
people for their material. So far, the main media of expression have been the
short poom and the short story, the latter usually with a strong touch of tragedy.
In this, as in other aspeots of Filipino striving, women take their place along
withmen.
So interosting is this literary movement thut it seems well worth sampling
some of these English-Filipino productions. In the next seotio: we can look over
several poems and sulections from prose writings. They havo been picked out, not
becauso their authors are neoossarily the leading writers, but because they seem
to reveal the spirit of this new literature.

The Value of Trudition
On the first day of every November we consider our ancestors and become
partioipants in the ritual of an old universal worship.
We think of the dead. We put flowers on their graves and we pray. We
review our Tradition and get drunk with devotion and remembrance.
Tradition, under this circumstanoe, deserves the dignity with which it is
invested.
It oasts upon us the oharm of experience, linking us to a Past and such
of its moments of nobility as human memory shall not let perish.
It fortifies us in our endeavors of the present and inspires the improve-
ment in detail of our dreams of the future.!:
It gives us, not bonds to chain us forover to ancient manners, but a
commission to contributo to the common store of human kindness so that life for us
and those to follow may dovelop an increasing distinction.
Devotion to the doad, symbol of Tradition, wo think, oan have no better
justifioation. Loyalty to them, interpreted in other terms, can only mean that we
care more for perpetuating the despotism of a cult than for preserving the back-
ground for a finer future.
