CHAPTER XVIII
FILIPINOS TRY OUT A GOLDEN TRAIL
In 1920, there were 27,000 Filipinos in the United States.
Ten years
later, the number had grown to more than 100,000.
. This total has fallen away
somewhat, because of the economic depression and other oiroumstances, but many
thousands have stayed on.
A scattering of Filipinos went abroad in Spanish days, mainly to Europe.
After 19o0, students wore sent with government soholarships to America. On their
return they told stories of good times and the case of carning one's way through
college, and this attracted others.
Today there are many Filipinos studying in
the universities.
But the main impetus to immigration oame through the sugar planters of
Hawaii.
As early as 1906-07, a group of Tagalogs from Manila were brought to
the Hewaiian plantations. But most of them soon drifted from there to the towns,
or else baok to their homes. Two years later, an arrangement was made with the
Philippine government for reoruiting to be done in the Visayan or central islands.
These Visayan peasants tended to be easy-going and improvident, and their ignorance
and the pugnacity shown by some of them gave a rather bad impression of the Fili-
pinos as a people.
During the Great War, permission was seoured to obtain laborers from the
orowded Ilocos country in north Luzon.
.The Ilocanos proved to be "milder," hard
working and thrifty. It is from here that the greut majority of later immigrants
have come. The Hawaiian Sugar Planters' Association joined with the steamship
companies in enlisting workers, though the attractive prospeots held out and the
glowing aocounts sent back by those who went, made it easy to get the required
volunteers. At first the fares of the laborers were paid. But after a time this

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
-128-:
beoame unnecessary; Filipinos were so anxious to get to this new land of promise
that they were willing to meet the expenses themselves.
The immigrants were nearly all young men. Exoept for some of the
earlier Tagalogs and Visayans, they came almost entirely without womenfolk.
The
terms of their contract provided that, if they remained on the plantations for
three years of sorvice, their passages home would be paid. In 1920 and 1924 the
Filipinos went on strike for higher wages. But on the whole plantation condi-
tions have been made sufficiently attractive to hold enough workers to carry on.
They have better housing than at home, special clubs, facilities for sport and
reoreation, and free medioal services.
Nevertheless, many of the laborers have been keen to see more of the
world than the plantation camp.
When their period of service expired, or before
that, they moved in to Honolulu city. Some got jobs as hotel boys, domestio
servants, musioians or the like. Others were content to work for a short period
eaoh year in the pineapple canneries there, and in the off-season to take it easy
around town. If they ran short of money for their simple needs, they were
supported by thoir friends.
Numbers of these former laborers were students ho
took this means of getting abroad for further education. Several of these, by
dint of hard work, now hold important business posts in Hawaii, aoting as inter-
mediaries between the local firms and the Filipino population.
Large numbers of these immigrants, huving saved enough for their fares,
moved on to the Pacific coast states. Others crossed direct to the mainland from
the Philippines, or else received their disohergo there after service in the
American navy and army.
. They became concentrated partioularly in California.
Those who could not get city jobs worked as manual laborers in fruit and vegetat
growing distriots where cheap labor was in demand.
Because the Filipino with his simpler standards of living could undercut

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.99)
-129-
the white worker. hostility was soon aroused. This has also happened there in
the case of Chinese, Japanese, and Mexican immigrants. Starting in 1929, a
series of olushes have occurred involving greater or less violence. The news-
papers still report them from time to time. Amerioan labor representatives came
to advocate strongly the exclusion of Filipinos from the United States, and have
therefore thrown their weight in favor of independenoe for the islands. The
Hawaiian employers, on the other hand, opposed such exclusion.
Among the poorer folk in the Philippines, caught in the dull round of
economic subservience, the reports of conditions in America sounded almost fabulous.
"The first great ontingent of Filipinos met with great
success. Their letters to their folk at home contained happy
news, together with muterial results in the form of prescnts

successes grew in volume and becamc a by-word in their home

"I do not blame our laborers for loaving the Philippines if
they think they can find better wages in other countries, but I

"I hope that  onditions here will become such as to offer

all to foster its economic development." 2
With the grent depression, the scene hes changed entirely. There has
been considerable unemployment and hardship among the Filipinos overseas. The
idea of America as a land of promise has become dissipated.

Planters paid the return fares of several thousands who have gone baok empty

They urged strongly that Filipinos stay ut home and develop their

By the terms of the independence act, all Filipinos not born or natural-
ized in the United States now have the status of "aliens," and are exoluded from
the mainland of the United States, exoept for on annual quota of fifty, together
with special people like diplomats, students, and temporary visitors. An exemption
is made for the Territory of Hawaii, where Filipinos may be admitted under the
supervision of the department of the interior should there be a demand for them
as industrial workers.
. Since the pussing of the act, Filipinos in the United
States have become rather concorned as to their position.
.Many are thoroughly
used to American conditions of life, and they fear that should they leave to visit
their homes and relatives they will be unable to return.
For this reeson, some
of them have been strongly against independence.
The Filipinos who come overseas are faced with many problems in adjust-
ing to their new life.
If they have been trained in the higher schools, they are,
of course, familiar with the American language und customs - at least from books.
But the vast majority oome from poor rural homes, and at most will have had a few
years in the elomentary sohools. Arriving on the Huwaiian plantation or in the
mainland city, they find themselves having to learn suddonly now modes of speech und
conduot.

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)
-131-
They must conform to a very different code of laws and manners from
that of their barrio life. Where formerly they were merely a unit of a family,
accustomed to taking orders from their elders or the oaoique, and having them
plan the daily round, they are now expeoted to behave as fully self-responsible
individuals. The men outnumber the women by about eleven to one, so that a
normal family and social life is denied to most of them. And they have money in
their pockets that at home would represent comparative wealth.
When all these facts are considered, the Filipinos in the United States
must on the whole be congratulated for their orderliness and progress. Compared
with certain otler laboring groups in the same position, the amount of crime and
delinquency among them has been small. Occasional fights or serious acts of
violence are usually the result of jealousy over the few womenfolk who are with
them. At times, there have been disagreements between the Ilocanos, the Taga-
logs, and the Visayans.
Or their love of cook-fighting and gambling, pro-
hibited on American soil, causes them to run foul of the law. Yet the Fili-
pinos do not often steal, as any who are in economic diffioulty are usually
supported by compatriots - that is, the traditional hospitality and cooperation
carries over. They are docile and willing workers, perhaps looking to their
employers in somewhat tho sume way as they hitherto looked to their superiors at
home.
Already it has been pointed out how money is sont back to the Philip-
pines.
This is ospecially so with the thrifty Ilocanos. Indeed, some em-
ployers complain that they do not spend enough on thoir food to keep them fit end
healthy.
. On the other hond, few Filipinos can resist indulging in gay clothes,
danoes, movies, boxing, baseball and other entertainments, including the native
cock-fighting - with, of course, some betting on the result. The psyohological

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
-132-
Filipinos also like to move from one job to another, or even to lay off when they
have money in their pockets.
Among themselves, Filipino groups in the United States have their own
forms of social activity - clubs, lodges, and even at times strange religious
sects. There are numbers of newspapers in English and the dialeots, though
these rarely are long-lived. Rizal day is always celebrated, and in this and
other ways the Filipinos show themselves to be very patriotic. Unfortunately,
those more ignorant or newly arrived have often fallen a prey to unscrupulous
fellow Filipinos. They huve been swindled with lotteries, beauty contests, and
other money-making schemes. Eut there are also numbers of Filipino religious
and social workers who are helping such newcomers to become adjusted. Like all
new imigrant groups, the Filipinos have not sottled down yet as a whole to a
stable community life. Their ways of living are a varied mingling of Filipino
and Amerioan customs.
Now that independence is in sight, it remains to be seen how many
Filipinos will resist the oall of the homeland and choose to settle down as Ameri-
cans.
The spirit of almost exaggerated nationalism born of their history is
against it.
. Nevertheless a number may well do so. Some have good positions,
and in certain instances they have married girls of other races. Furthermore, an
increasing group of Filipino children have beon porn in the United States and
hence are full American citizens.
They tend to be assimilated into Amerioan life,
and the stories they may have heard their parents tell of poverty and struggle in
the ancestral barrios do not serve to attract them away. Even when the Philip-
pines become independent, therefore, there will still be numbers of people of
Filipino blood within the American nation. The lin!: between the two countries
in this as in other ways will not be wholly severod.

CHAPTER XIX
THE TRENDS OF NATIDI BUILDING
Our journey among the Philippine islands and their people now draws to
a close.
. In the brief time and spaoe at our disposal we have reviewed the his-
tory and present life of the Filipinos, likewise something of the problems of
nation building that face them in the future.
The Filipinos, we have seen, draw for their physical and mental heri-
tage upon the racial stuff of praotically all mankind. Ve may credit them with
being on the average under no biological handicap us a people. Given the oppor-
tunity, increasing numbers of Filipinos are mastering the civilization of the West,
foreign as it has boen to them us folk of Eastern buckgrounds. Where Filipinos
may seem by our standards buckward, ignorant, emotionally unstable, flashy, easy
going, supor-sensitive and so on, we can appreciate the oultural differences and
historical experienoes which have produced suoh characteristics. It would be
false for us to speak of these as pormanent inborn marks, the more so as forces
are today at work changing vastly the Filipino life and thought.
We have noticod espocially that the Filipino people are rooted in ono
or another of their many local heritagos, yet ut the same time responding to a
wider sense of citizenship. The two kinds of linkage, regional and national,
seem in no way at odds. They oan be adjustcd successfully to cach other so as t
produce a rich Philippine lifo. A recent governor-general, Theodore Roosevelt,
writes of this:
"Individuals in a community differ very greatly one from
another, and still are able through holding tc generul broad
concepts whioh they huve in common to work together cohesively
us a community.
What holds true of individuuls holds true of
groups as woll.
. Sufficient similarity in beliefs and customs
cun be attained to pormit of integrution as a part of a united

The above was written with special reference to the adjustment of the
non-Christian peoplcs into the general Philippine life. In this connection, it
is a rather striking fact that Christian Filipinos and many Americans have for
years been advocating a policy of quiok "assimilation" for the pagan and Mohammeden
Filipinos. Yet they have not defined what type of Filipino they wish to make of
them. The older non-Christian vays of life have been rather systematically
attaoked as undesirable, and the children in the schools have been encouraged more
or less openly to break away. Nowadays, however, a few people arc beginning to
realize there is no gencral type of Filipino life into which the non-Christians
could be absorbed. At most they oould be mude into imitation Ilocanos, Visayans
or other Christians. So far, the pagan and Mohummedan groups have remained con-
servative, and their anoient ideas and customs are largely kept to. The new con-
ception of assimilating the non-Christien is not to destroy but to build upon
these local heritages, es hes huppened with the Christians. Time and the voluntary
choice of the people themsolves will then show what is end what is not worth pre-
serving of the old ways, and confliots will be reduced to the minimum.
There is a general question of grent interest concerning the future of
the Filipino people as a whole. Now that tho tonds with the United States ure
apparently being severed, will the new nation continue to develop its prosent
contaots with the Occident or turn more toward the Orient? A Filipino educator
writes:
"The Filipino is in a most peculiar situation. Geographi-
cally and racially, he belongs to the East, but culturally he
belongs partly to the Wost... It can only now be a matter of
speculation whether it would have been allowod to dovelop thoir
own native civilizution as the Chinese und Japanese unin-
terfered with by westorn powers and influence. " 2

Francisoo Benitcz, Educational Progress in the Philippines. Institute of

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
-135-
He himself, after woighing the gains and losses of the modern period, concludes:
"I have no regrets thut the Filipino of four nundred years ago has changed into
the Filipino of today." Nevertheless he and others see that for the future there
is the alternative of emphasizing either things Oocidental or things Oriental.
Some years ago, Professor Beyer of the University of the Philippines
pointed out that Americuns were assuming a heavy responsibility in superimposing
Western ways of speeoh, thought and custom upon the Filipinos and so turning them
more and more from tho Eust. Certuinly today, the educated Filipino does not show
any enthusiasm or attraction towards the ways of his Oriental neighbors. His
attitudes towurd the Japunese, for example, are much like those of the average
American. Ho fears their cconomic and political strength, and dislikes their
penetration of his country. Furthermore, the filipinos are overwhelmingly
Christian - and often very pious - so that tho Chinose, and even other Malayans
are looked down upon as "infideIs "
Short of an upsurging of the older Malayanism
among the masses, whose lives still bear predominantly the sooial murks of th:
Orient, Ocoidentalized minds and manners seem likoly to remain at the helm. Yet
it is upon Asiatic waters thut tho new nation is being launched.
One thing seems clear, however.
Vhutever type of national life is
worked out, it will be essentially a Filipino life. along with the urge for
political freedom, there has been emerging a spirit of "Filipinism". The Fili-
pinos of the future will not bo willing to ape the ideas and customs of any out-
side people. Whether they lean meinly to East or West, they will make whatever
they take over their own. Amerioa has copied no Europoun nation exactly while yet
drawing upon many, and so it will be with the Philippines. The Islands and their
people are today in the proccss of becoming modernized, and in doing so are fusing
valuable elements fror both th old Asian background. and the new Wostern civiliza-
tion.

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.99)
-136-
Some Filipino thinkers, indeed, have seen this not only as enriohing
for themselves but also as giving their people a real mission to perform in the
ohanging world of today. They think of the Philippines as offering an experi-
mental bridge by which East and West can meet. Dean M. M. Kalaw, of the college
of liberal arts in the University of the Philippines writes:
"Wc have thus u most strategic cultural position - an Oriental
peoplo standing at the portals of Asia in deep sympathy with
their kindred neighbors yet with hands outstretohed to the cul-
tures of Spain nd America. Wo still relish with pleasure the
chivalry and courtesy that our ancient metropolis has bequeathed
to us; at the same time we aro holding on to imerioa's ideal of
democracy, her business methods, her efficiency, her high stand-
ards for health und sanitation, her espousal of maohine methods,
and her idea of popular eduoation. We owe it to our posterity
and to thc world to effeot a harmonious blending of all these cul-
tural cloments. Under the aegis of freedom we expeot to evolve
a mode of life adapted to the modern age yet sufficiently Asiatic
in tho feeling of comradeship with our Oriental nations. We plan
to continue retaining a personality of our own and to dovelop the
good traits of our people. Our hospitality, our generosity, our
respect for the elders, our peace-loving nature, and our artistic
bents, we intend to preserve. Our chief charaoteristic should be
our catholioity; our chief strength the harmonious amalgamation
of tho East nnd the West." 3
If these idealistio words can be in some measure fulfilled, the Philippines
will indeed be a not unimportant unit in the world community. While today we
cannot see very clearly cither the diruction of internal developments or the likely
trends of external affairs in the Far East upon both of whioh the destiny of the
Filipino people depends, wc oen watch with sympathy their struggle to develop as a
nation, and we oan wish then well in tho task.
